- **Grouped by event window, not a flat card list.** The handoff's own *Superseded decisions* retires the flat list for two reasons it names as problems found in the built version: four cards all reading "4.0★" never say *which event* they are for, and a sunrise-only suggestion is an easy dismissal when the same region's sunset is the Best Bet. Windows go **chronologically** (the user is deciding *when*); cards within a window go by **rating** (the *when* is settled, they are choosing *where*). Distance gates but never sorts — it is on every card already.
- **The two signal flags**, which the handoff calls the highest-value part of the feature. `NOT IN THE BRIEFING` — the region reads STANDDOWN region-wide while a nearby location still rates GO; this is the case the region-level grid structurally *cannot* show. `SAME WINDOW AS BEST BET` — the local option is not a compromise. Both computed, never hardcoded.
- **Per-user local radius (V136)**, 10–50 miles, default 22. Nullable with no backfill so the column distinguishes "left alone" from "deliberately set to 22". No new endpoint — it rides the existing `PUT /api/user/settings/home`, because the radius is measured from that home. Out-of-range values are clamped rather than rejected; a null leaves the stored value alone rather than resetting it.
- Cards now carry the location's **subject-type icons**, from the same `locationTypes` source of truth the map and admin use.
- The endpoint returns **facts, not prose**: day chips, formatted times and the breadcrumb sentence are assembled on the client. The logic needed one home; the copy did not.
- The breadcrumb still **never names a nearby location on a stay-in night** — an earlier iteration surfaced one at 1.8★, which undercut the verdict beside it.

### Added — `GET /api/briefing/close-to-home`, the Close to home derivation moved server-side
- **The join is why it was worth doing.** The client matched briefing slots to the locations roster by NAME, so renaming a location silently emptied the panel for every user inside the radius. This joins on `locationId`, falling back to the name only for cached payloads written before that field existed.
- **Its own contract, unlike its Plan-tab neighbours.** The other panels are views of the same shared forecast snapshot and ride one `GET /api/briefing`, because two panels fetching independently can disagree about the same location. This one answers a different question about *differently owned* data — the caller's home postcode and their own drive times — which is the only thing that earns a separate endpoint.
- **Never ETag-revalidated**, and now pinned: an ETag forces `Cache-Control: private, no-cache`, which persists the body to a browser HTTP cache JavaScript cannot evict on logout. `HttpCachingConfigTest.personalDataPathsAreNeverFiltered` gained this path — it sits *under* `/api/briefing`, which **is** revalidated, so it is the entry that proves the whitelist's exact-match semantics do real work rather than just reading well.
- **Structured, not prose.** Day chips, formatted times and the breadcrumb sentence stay on the client; the endpoint returns the facts they are built from. The logic needed one home, the wording did not.
- **The freshness concern turned out not to exist.** The client preferred a separately-fetched evaluation-score map over the payload's own `claudeRating`, which looked like a merge worth reproducing. `BriefingService.getCachedBriefingForApi` already runs `enrichWithCachedScores` on the **serve** path from the same `getCachedScores` source, so taking the enriched payload yields exactly the ratings the Plan grid shows.
- `RADIUS_MILES` is now `photocast.close-to-home.radius-miles` — the precondition for it ever becoming a user setting, which a client constant made impossible.
- Mutation-checked: joining on name only, dropping the radius gate, and removing the card cap each fail their own test.

### Fixed — `GeoUtils.distanceMiles`
- Great-circle distance in **miles**, deliberately not the metres used elsewhere in that class: the only caller is a user-facing "22 miles" radius, and converting at the boundary puts a unit change between the configured number and the comparison — which is where a factor-of-1.6 bug hides.

### Added — `BriefingSlot.locationId`, so the briefing can be joined on the FK instead of the name
- **Close to home matches briefing slots to the locations roster by NAME string.** Rename a location in the admin UI and the block silently empties for every user inside the radius — no error, no empty state, the location simply stops existing as far as proximity is concerned. The FK has been available since **V47**; the briefing DTO just never carried it.
- Documented in `plan-panel-data-contracts.md` Section 5 as *"the FK already exists"* — which understates it. `BriefingSlot` is serialised into `daily_briefing_cache`, so this is a **payload-contract change**, not a lookup swap.
- **Nullable on purpose.** Every payload written before this field existed deserialises with a null id, and those entries keep being served until their key ages out. Consumers must treat the id as an *upgrade* on the name — prefer it when present, fall back to the name when absent — rather than a replacement.
- Both producers (`BriefingSlotBuilder`'s sky and canopy paths) already held the `LocationEntity`, so both now stamp `loc.getId()`. The id-less constructor is retained because **59 test call sites** use it, which means nothing at compile time stops a producer quietly dropping the id and reinstating the name join — so a test pins it instead.
- Mutation-checked: removing the id from either producer fails `bothProducers_stampLocationId`; dropping it in `withClaudeScores` fails both wither tests. A wither is exactly where an id goes quietly missing.
- Forward-compatibility pinned directly: a legacy JSON payload with no `locationId` deserialises to null rather than throwing (otherwise the briefing would 500 for every cached region the moment this shipped), and a null id is omitted from the serialised payload rather than written as `null`.

### Fixed — a `docker compose pull` between releases could abort the next deploy
- **`pre-release-backup.sh` reads the outgoing images' `RepoDigest` off the running containers**, so a rollback can pin exact builds rather than `:latest`. On 2026-07-29 that read came back **empty** and aborted the v2.17.3 deploy: `could not read image digests (backend='' ...)`. The trigger was a `docker compose pull` run between releases, which moved `:latest`.
- **The mechanism is deliberately not asserted.** It could not be reproduced cleanly afterwards — the host runs the containerd image store, where the image ID and repo digest are the same value, and the displaced image later showed its `RepoDigests` intact. So this guards the *symptom*: whatever the cause, an empty read stops a release, and the digest is reliably knowable at exactly one moment — just after the deploy that started the container.
- `scripts/record-deployed-images.sh` writes that digest to a ledger after each successful deploy, and `digest_of()` falls back to it. **The fallback returns a digest only when the ledger's recorded image ID still equals the running one**, so it is a checkable claim about the image actually running rather than an assumption about the last one. On a mismatch the backup aborts exactly as it does today — a manifest pinning the wrong build is worse than no manifest.
- Recording is **non-fatal throughout**: it runs after the new images are live, so a failure there would report a healthy deploy as broken. A missing ledger costs only the *next* release its fallback.
- Verified against the live production host: ledger write, recovery from a simulated empty read, and the mismatch guard rejecting a tampered ledger.

## [v2.17.3] - 2026-07-29

### Added — a wood finally gets its own rating, year-round
- **A canopy site out of bluebell season produced no Claude task at all.** It reached `DispositionCategory.SKIPPED_NO_PROMPT`, whose text read *"awaiting the woodland prompt"*, and showed a deterministic verdict with no rating for ~10.5 months of the year. This is that prompt.
- **Its polarity is the inverse of the sky prompt, deliberately.** Heavy overcast is *good* — a softbox over the whole wood. Broken cloud with the sun behind it is the *worst* case, dapple no exposure holds. Mist is the prize, and fog thick enough to resolve nothing is marked down. Strong wind is a **safety** matter, not an aesthetic one. It reports low+mid **combined** cover rather than the sky prompt's low/mid/high average, because high cirrus does not soften light at ground level — averaging reports 56% for a canopy that is in fact fully overcast.
- **Season-aware, and honest about what it cannot see.** Spring translucent green → summer flat dark → autumn peak → winter bare graphics, hedging *leaf state* the way the bluebell prompt hedges bloom: the date is known, the wood's condition on the day is not. Recent rain is an input (`precedingPrecipitationMm`, already feeding the bluebell `postRain` flag) bounded to the *hours* before the slot — the right window for wet-bark saturation — and the prompt is told that bound so it cannot infer a lush or drought-stricken wood it was never shown. A null prints `not available`, not `0.00`; those are different claims.
- **One lane per slot, never both.** Bluebell in season, woodland out of it. That exclusivity is what keeps each batch bucket homogeneous, which is what keeps its system prompt **cached** — the app's largest cost. Pinned by tests: the woodland system block carries ephemeral `cache_control`, is byte-identical across requests built from different atmospheric data, and is distinct from both the bluebell and sky prompts.
- **The triage exemption is not optional.** Sky triage stands a slot down at >80% low cloud and <5 km visibility — precisely the overcast and the mist a wood wants. Without it the feature would be born inverted, triaged out on every good woodland morning.
- **Separate `WoodlandEvaluation` and `WoodlandVisitor` despite an identical JSON shape**, because every canopy site in production is *also* typed `BLUEBELL` with an exposure: `BluebellVisitor.appliesTo()` matches all 15, so a shared carrier would file a woodland response as a `BLUEBELL` component. The rating would look right and the provenance would be a lie.
- Adds **V135** (seeds `forecast_type` id 7, insert-only). Adversarially reviewed: 19 findings raised, 7 confirmed, 12 refuted; the largest is released separately above.

### Fixed — CI never ran on a stacked PR, and the silence looked like a pass
- **`pull_request: branches: [main]` means "only PRs whose BASE is main".** A stacked PR — one opened against another feature branch so a large change can be reviewed and rolled back in pieces — never matches it, so neither `ci.yml` nor `codeql.yml` ran.
- **And nothing else caught it**, because branch protection's required checks apply to `main`: a PR targeting any other branch has no required checks either. The result is a merge path where *nothing runs and nothing objects*.
- That is how **#361**, a prompt/batching change, merged into its parent branch with **zero CI**. Nothing broke — the branch had been verified locally first, and the combined result went green on #360 before reaching main — but the gate was *silent*, not satisfied, and on the PR page those look identical.
- Both triggers are now unfiltered by base branch. The cost is one extra run when a stacked PR is later retargeted at main; the alternative is an ungated merge path. The `push` triggers stay pinned to `main`, so branch pushes do not double up.

### Changed — Map legend names the product, not the model
- The map's bottom legend read **"★ Claude-scored locations shown"**, naming the vendor behind the scoring rather than the thing the user is looking at. It now reads **"★ PhotoCast-scored locations shown"**.
- Which model produced a score is an implementation detail that has already changed more than once (Haiku/Sonnet/Opus per run type, and the batch near/far split). The legend's job is to distinguish scored pins from unscored ones, and that distinction is stable regardless of what runs underneath.
- The `data-testid` moved with it (`claude-scored-legend` → `photocast-scored-legend`) so the hook does not outlive the label it names. Nothing referenced either string, so no tests or other call sites changed.
- Copy elsewhere that names Claude *because the topic is the model* — the Manage tab's model selection, cost estimates, health probes — is deliberately untouched.

### Fixed — a region's cache entry was replaced by whichever batch finished last
- **The cache key is per REGION; the buckets are per BATCH.** `isCoastal` is a per-*location* tide test, so a region holding both coastal and inland sites emits tasks into two different sky batches carrying the **same** cache key. `writeFromBatch` rebuilds the entry from only the incoming batch's results and replaces both the in-memory cache and `cached_evaluation.results_json` wholesale — `persistToDb` does a straight `setResultsJson`, no merge. Whichever batch finished second silently deleted the first's locations.
- **Live in production, and not new.** Three regions mix coastal and inland enabled locations — Northumberland **33/43**, The North Yorkshire Coast **16/9**, Tyne and Wear **2/9** — so one half of each has been discarded on every cycle since the coastal/inland split was introduced. The paid-for evaluations were made, written, and then thrown away.
- **The sync path was worse.** `handleSyncResult` passed `writeFromBatch` a *single* result, so one location's evaluation replaced its entire region.
- **Always merge now, on both paths.** `mergeFromBatch` already overlays per location name onto the prior entry (falling back to the DB) — it is what the retry path has used for exactly this reason since it was written. Merging costs a stale location lingering in a region entry until that key ages out; replacing costs a paid-for evaluation. The trade is not close.
- `flushCacheKey` is deprecated with the reason on it, and retained **only** so the destructive behaviour stays directly testable — the new tests pin both directions: two merges on one key keep both locations, and `writeFromBatch` on the same key demonstrably loses the first. The defect is proven, not just the fix.
- Found by an adversarial review of unrelated work; five of seven review lenses landed on it independently.

### Changed — one source of truth for location types, and two harnesses that had drifted from it

- **The type map lived in five copies**, and adding WOODLAND cost five hand-edits to keep them in step. `MapView.LOCATION_TYPE_LABELS`, `LocationTypeBadges.LOCATION_TYPE_META`, `MarkerPopupContent.POPUP_LOC_TYPE_META`, `briefingDisplay.LOCATION_TYPE_ICONS` and `LocationManagementView.LOCATION_TYPES` now all derive from `utils/locationTypes.js`.
- **They had already drifted.** `briefingDisplay` used 💧 for WATERFALL where the other four used 💦, and no two of the five agreed on ordering. Deriving the icon lookup from the metadata makes that unrepresentable rather than merely fixed.
- **The drift was silent by construction**, which is why it survived: every consumer either filters unknown types out or falls back to the raw enum name, so a missing constant renders as nothing and never throws. A test now asserts the metadata covers every backend `LocationType`.
- **Two harnesses were excluding waterfall locations.** `ModelTestView` (two sites) and `PromptTestView` tested `LANDSCAPE || SEASCAPE` and omitted WATERFALL, so a waterfall location could not be picked in the model-comparison or prompt-test rosters even though `LocationEntity.hasColourTypes()` — the gate the backend engines actually filter on — admits it. All three predicates now call the shared `isSkyPromptCandidate`, which mirrors that gate exactly, including its rule that an untyped location counts. **This widens both rosters**; it aligns them with the backend and with what #355's own notes said they already did.
- **`isSkyPromptCandidate` is deliberately not the briefing's candidacy test.** "May this go to the sky prompt?" and "is this a candidate for the briefing?" have different answers for a wood, and conflating them is the bug the backend records at `LocationEntity.hasColourTypes()`. The module says so where someone would reach for the wrong one.
- **BLUEBELL has metadata but no permanent chip or badge** (`DISPLAY_TYPES`): a bluebell site is a seasonal *subject*, not a kind of place, so a year-round badge would assert a display that is true for a few weeks. The map already worked this way — a BLUEBELL chip appears only while the bloom is on. It still gets a proper label wherever it *is* surfaced.
- Behaviour-preserving otherwise, and verified so: the WOODLAND filter-chip tests from #355 still fail if WOODLAND is dropped from the shared list.

### Fixed — a superseded triage row could veto a live rating and empty the map

- **The two score sources disagree by design, and the map read both.** `/api/briefing/evaluate/scores` prefers a cached evaluation (`EvaluationViewService.mergeToView` step 1) while `/api/forecast` returns the latest run per slot, so one slot can hold a live cached rating *and* a superseded triaged forecast row. `MapView` ORed `triageReason` across both, so the stale row won.
- **The veto ran before the quality filter**, so no setting could recover the marker: `isStandDownLocation` is the first predicate in the rating filter and returns `showStandDown`, which is off by default. A 4★ location vanished from the map while the Plan grid, Best Bet and the map-peek narrative all still read "Worth it" — the narrative path never consults `triageReason` at all.
- **The rule is now symmetric**, in a shared `resolveStandDown` (`utils/standDown.js`): **a rating from either source beats a `triageReason` from the other.** That makes it the exact complement of the rating lookup — a slot stands down precisely when `briefingScore?.rating ?? forecast?.rating` is null and something triaged it. An asymmetric fix would have corrected one direction and left the mirror image.
- **`MarkerPopupContent` shared the helper too**, because it answered the same question a third way: its forecast branch consulted only `forecast.triageReason`, so once the marker was fixed a scored medallion would have opened a popup reading "Stand-down". Marker and popup now derive rating and verdict identically.
- **Deliberately not diagnosed here:** this makes the map self-consistent, but whether a given empty map is *this* bug or genuinely triaged data is a question for the payload, not the code.

### Fixed — the map overlay's drill-down emphasis had never worked

- **`makeMarkerIcon` computed the `focus`/`muted` modifier, folded it into the icon cache key, and then built every `DivIcon` with a bare `photocast-marker` class.** The `.photocast-marker--focus` / `--muted` rules in `popupStyles` matched nothing, so the "lift the target, recede its neighbours" treatment shipped inert in v2.17.0 — no error, no warning, no test.
- **Making it live exposed a second bug**, so the modifier is now gated on the target surviving the filters. Nothing relaxes the star or stand-down thresholds for a handoff, so drilling into a spot the filter hides left *every* remaining marker muted at 40% opacity in deference to a pin that was not on the map.
- New `MapViewMarkerEmphasis.test.jsx` asserts on the className handed to `L.divIcon` — the only observable the behaviour depends on, and one every existing map test stubs away.

### Fixed — a test that failed for two hours every night

- `NlcSightingBanner`'s "Xh ago" test derived its fixture as "two hours ago" from the real clock, so between 00:00 and 01:59 local that landed on the previous calendar day and `formatReportedAt` correctly returned "yesterday HH:MM". The component was right; the test now owns its clock.

### Fixed — editing a location silently dropped all but one of its types
- **The entity has always stored a set; the request carried a scalar.** `AddLocationRequest` and `UpdateLocationRequest` took a single `LocationType`, and `LocationService` expanded it with `Set.of(...)` — a full replace. Opening a location in the admin editor reduced its types to `set[0]`, and saving persisted that. **Editing only a location's name dropped every other type.**
- **Which type survived was arbitrary.** The set arrives from a Java `HashSet`, whose iteration order is not stable — so the same location could lose a different type on a different day.
- **Already live, independent of woodland.** Production carries 263 type rows across 242 locations, so roughly **21 locations hold two or more types** today. It also took tide preferences with it: the editor keys its tide rules off the collapsed scalar, so a `[SEASCAPE, WOODLAND]` site collapsing the wrong way was sent `tideTypes: []`.
- **And it undoes the woodland work on contact.** Dropping `WOODLAND` from one of the 15 woods puts it straight back on sky scoring — the thing V132 and #349 existed to stop.
- Both records now take `Set<LocationType>`, matching their own `solarEventTypes`/`tideTypes` siblings. An **empty set is rejected**, not ignored: silently keeping the old types would hide a broken UI, and silently writing an empty set would drop the location out of every evaluation lane at once — invisible until nothing was ever forecast for it again.
- **Two further defects found on the way.** `UpdateLocationRequest` coerced a null `tideTypes` to `Set.of()` in its canonical constructor, making the `!= null` guard in `update()` permanently true — so a caller that merely *omitted* the field had a coastal location's tide preferences **wiped** rather than left alone, the exact opposite of the "null = don't change" contract the record documents. And the SEASCAPE test is now `contains()` rather than equality, so a wooded clifftop keeps its tides.
- Tide seeding fires only on an actual coastal transition; re-seeding on every change would wipe a curated preference the moment an unrelated chip was added.
- Every new test was **mutation-checked** — but the first pass was **incomplete, and said otherwise**. It mutated the `update()` path and the request record and never `add()`, so both of `add()`'s changed lines could be reverted with the whole suite green; the test believed to cover the equality→`contains()` swap was in fact pinning pre-existing `update()` behaviour, which had always used `contains()`. An adversarial review caught it along with two regressions the change itself introduced (see the entry below). Eleven mutations now cover both paths.

### Fixed — a partial update could strand a location as permanently coastal
- Found by adversarial review of the change above, and **introduced by it**. Preserving null in `UpdateLocationRequest`'s canonical constructor was right, but it removed the only enforcement of *"no tides unless SEASCAPE"*: the tide block runs only when the caller supplies `tideTypes`.
- **`isCoastal()` is a non-empty tide set, not the SEASCAPE type.** So a partial update that dropped SEASCAPE without mentioning tides left them behind, and the now-inland location stayed coastal forever — billed WorldTides fetches, and `BriefingSlotBuilder` overriding its verdict to STANDDOWN on a tide alignment it can never satisfy. Now re-derived whenever `locationTypes` was supplied, scoped to that so a legacy non-SEASCAPE row carrying tides is not blanked by an edit that never touched types.
- Also fixed: `htmlFor` binds only to labelable elements, so replacing the add form's `<select>` with a chip `<div>` left an inert label (`span` + `role="group"` + `aria-labelledby`, applied to the two sibling chip groups that had the same defect already); and a **pre-existing vacuous test** whose exact-match `queryByText` used a *prefix* of the real message — `'...tide preference'` against the rendered `'...tide preference (High, Mid, or Low).'` — so it returned null whether or not the error was displayed and could never fail.

### Added — the frontend learns about WOODLAND
- `grep -ri woodland frontend/src` returned **nothing** outside test fixtures. Five display maps enumerate location types and none listed it, so the 15 woods arriving with the v2.17.2 deploy had no icon, no badge, no popup label and no map filter.
- **The map filter was the damaging one.** `LOCATION_TYPE_LABELS` drives both the chip row *and*, through `activeTypeFilters`, the filter predicate — so a type absent from that map can never be a member of the active set, and a location carrying only that type matched nothing. Selecting **any** Subject chip made every woodland-only site vanish, with no chip that could bring it back. That reads as *"those locations are missing"*, not *"those locations are filtered out"*.
- Deliberately **not** changed: the type *predicates* in `PromptTestView`, `ModelTestView` and `JobRunsMetricsView` omit WOODLAND on purpose, mirroring the backend's `hasColourTypes()` — a wood must not appear in a colour-model test roster. Nor `MarkerPopupContent`'s `"Bluebell:"` label, which is gated on `bluebellScore` and genuinely describes bluebell conditions; a woodland line belongs with the woodland prompt, when there is a woodland score to show.

### Fixed — the rollback record understated the schema version, in the dangerous direction
- **`MAX(version)` on Flyway's history table compares text, not numbers.** `version` is `VARCHAR`, so with V99 and V134 both applied `MAX()` returns **`'99'`** — `'9' > '1'`. Verified against production: the broken query returns `99`, the corrected one `134`. The bug has been live since V100 landed, and all five rollback manifests on the box record `"schema_version": "99"`.
- **It fails in the direction that reassures.** This number exists to answer *"does undoing this release need the dump restored, or only the images re-pinned?"* Understating it makes a rollback look cheaper than it is. Tonight's v2.17.2 release logged `schema version: 99` while production was on **130** — a gap spanning V132, which carries an irreversible `DELETE` and a `DROP COLUMN`.
- **`rollback.sh` was the worse of the two, because both of its readings were wrong the same way.** They therefore agreed with each other and the post-restore check passed, while the `ROLLBACK PLAN` banner printed identical before/after versions — telling the operator the restore was schema-neutral at precisely the moment they were deciding whether to discard production data. Two independently-wrong values would have been noticed; two consistently-wrong ones were not.
- Severity is bounded by the value being **display-only** — nothing branches on it — so the damage is a misinformed human decision, not automated data loss.
- Fixed at all three call sites (`pre-release-backup.sh`, `rollback.sh` ×2) by ordering on the numeric version with `installed_rank` as tiebreak, hoisted to one `SCHEMA_VERSION_SQL` constant per script so the three cannot drift apart again. The correct idiom already existed in `IntegrationTestBaseSmokeTest`; the scripts never picked it up.

## [v2.17.2] - 2026-07-28

### Fixed — #347 put canopy sites back in the sky lane, and let their verdicts vote in the sky roll-up
- **Found by a retrospective adversarial review of #347/#348**, which were merged before the rule that batch/prompt/scoring/triage changes get one. Both defects are **real and confirmed in code**, and neither has reached production: prod is on **V130**, so V131–V134 have never run and no location is typed `WOODLAND` yet. They would have landed on the next deploy.
- **`hasColourTypes()` is the shared gate for the SKY prompt** — `ForecastCommandExecutor`, `ForceSubmitBatchService`, `ModelTestService` and `PromptTestService` all filter on it. #347 added `|| contains(WOODLAND)` to it, which silently undid V132: those woods had been removed from the sky lane by losing `LANDSCAPE`, and this put them straight back. Every engine would have sent them to the fiery-sky prompt — the **opposite** of what #347's own commit message claimed (*"deliberately no rating and no model call"*). The arm is reverted; woods reach the briefing through `BriefingService.isColourLocation`, a deliberately separate predicate. *"Is this a briefing candidate"* and *"may this go to the sky prompt"* are different questions, and conflating them caused the leak.
- **Canopy verdicts were voting in the sky region roll-up.** `BriefingHierarchyBuilder.buildRegion` passed every slot to `rollUpVerdict`, which fires GO at 20% of viable slots — so one overcast wood could carry a whole region to "Worth it", and `buildRegionSummary` would then read literally **"Clear at 1 of 5 locations"** while counting a slot that is GO *because* it is overcast. That contaminated verdict also reached the Best Bet model raw, at build time, ahead of the read-path honesty filter.
- **Only sky slots now vote.** Woods keep their own slot, verdict and flags in the drill-down — they simply do not vote on a question they are not answering. A region with nothing but canopy slots falls back to them rather than having no verdict.
- **The root cause was a stated design premise.** #347 said *"everything downstream needs no knowledge of the fork"*. That is the bug: `Verdict.GO` now means two opposite things and nothing carries which. The partition is threaded from `buildDays`, which already has the roster; a durable fix is a subject discriminator on `BriefingSlot`, deferred because 60 construction sites use its canonical constructor.
- The roll-up test asserts the **premise as well as the fix** — without the partition the wood does carry the region — so it proves the defect was real rather than only that the new code works.
### Fixed — Canopy sites were sent to the sky prompt, and selected for it backwards
- **There were two sky gates and #347 only forked one.** `BriefingVerdictEvaluator` feeds the Plan grid and now has a woodland sibling; `WeatherTriageEvaluator` decides whether Claude is called at all, shares the same thresholds (cloud 80 / visibility 5000), and had **zero** `LocationType` awareness.
- **So the selection was inverted, not merely imprecise.** Out of bluebell season a wood was triaged **out** on the misty overcast morning it wants, and passed **through** on the clear day it does not — where it was then rated for fiery-sky potential a location under a canopy structurally cannot have. Money spent on wrong answers, on exactly the wrong days.
- **A canopy site now produces no sky task, in or out of season.** Submitting nothing is better than submitting the wrong prompt, and the grid still shows these sites — the deterministic verdict from `WoodlandVerdictEvaluator` (#347) — so they stay visible; they simply carry no Claude rating or prose until the woodland prompt lands.
- **Narrow by design**: a wood that also carries an open type keeps its sky task, because it has a horizon and the sky evaluation is the one that decides whether to drive there. Allen Banks is the case.
- **In-season behaviour is untouched** — a woodland bluebell site still goes to the bluebell bucket alone, exactly as before.
- Recorded as `SKIPPED_TRIAGED` with the reason *"Canopy site — no sky evaluation; awaiting the woodland prompt"*, so the disposition trail says why rather than looking like an ordinary triage.
- Both rules mutation-verified: removing the skip, or widening it to catch woods that also have an aspect, each turns exactly the right test red.
- **Unblocked, and the root cause fixed rather than routed around.** The retrospective review found that once canopy slots can never carry a rating, `BriefingHonestyFilter` would read a wood-bearing region as zero-coverage and blank it daily with *"conditions too unsettled to evaluate"* — a lie twice over, since nothing failed and a per-location forecast **does** exist. That filter is failure-defence: its premise is *"we expected Claude coverage and did not get it"*, and a canopy slot never expected any. Coverage is now measured against **scoreable** slots, so an all-canopy region passes through untouched while a genuine batch failure is still blanked.
- **`BriefingSlot` gained a `canopy` component**, which is the fix the review actually asked for. Three services were separately resolving a "which locations are woods" roster — the roll-up, the confidence denominator and now the honesty filter — because a `Verdict` alone is ambiguous once two evaluators with **opposite polarity** produce one. The subject now travels with the slot. Cheaper than feared: 61 of 62 construction sites use the convenience constructor, so only the record and one call site changed, and a named `BriefingSlot.canopySlot(...)` factory means the one place that produces them cannot silently stop marking them.

- **Amended after adversarial review**, before merge:
  - **Coverage denominator no longer counts unscoreable slots.** A canopy site has no sky evaluation by construction, so leaving it in `rosterSize` made `ConfidenceDeriver`'s thin-coverage rule downgrade a wood-heavy region's confidence one band **every day, permanently** — including the landscape sites in that region that have nothing to do with woods. The canopy rosters are resolved once per enrichment call, not per slot: this path runs on every serve over ~1,900 slots, so a lookup inside the loop would have been ~1,900 queries per request.
  - **New `SKIPPED_NO_PROMPT` disposition** instead of reusing `SKIPPED_TRIAGED`, whose javadoc means "rejected by triage". These candidates *pass* triage — conflating them would report slots as triaged that were never triaged, corrupting the metric. Frontend `DispositionBreakdown` gains the row.
  - **The skip is counted.** It previously incremented nothing, so `included + skipped` silently stopped reconciling against the candidate count by ~48 a cycle. Now `canopy-no-prompt=` in the `[BATCH DIAG]` line.
  - Disposition uses `candidateDaysAhead` (injected clock, Europe/London) rather than `preEval.daysAhead()` (UTC), matching its sibling triage disposition.
- **A second review of the amended head caught that the amendment reverted its own predecessor.** Replacing the roster lookup with a flat `!slot.canopy()` filter silently dropped the in-season carve-out added one commit earlier — so a canopy bluebell site scored by the bluebell prompt landed in the coverage **numerator but not the denominator**, pushing the ratio above 1 and disabling the lightly-evaluated warning in the class whose entire job is not overstating coverage. Both denominators now key on **what the slot carries** (`canopy && rating == null`) rather than on what it is, which needs no roster and no clock and cannot drift between the two sites. Pinned by a test using a *scored* canopy slot — the case that was entirely unpinned.
- **Corrected a claim in this entry's earlier draft.** It named `ForecastCommandExecutor` and `ForceSubmitBatchService` as open gaps; both filter on `hasColourTypes()`, which #350 made WOODLAND-free, so both were already closed. The genuinely unforked path was `collectRegionFilteredBatches`, and it now skips canopy sites unconditionally — that lane has no bluebell bucket to route them to.
- **`[ZERO COVERAGE]` no longer fires for all-canopy regions.** Its message asserts a rewrite that no longer happens, and it is the line an operator greps for a real overnight batch failure; firing it per region per event on every serve would drown that.

### Changed — Woods are briefed at dawn only
- **Mist forms overnight and burns off through the morning**, so a canopy location is at its best in the hours after sunrise and nearly flat from mid-morning until dusk. Scoring a sunset column for a wood manufactures a "best evening hour" the subject does not have, so the column is now left empty rather than filled with fiction.
- **The sampled hour needed no change.** `TimeSlotUtils.findBestIndex` already picks the slot at or just *after* sunrise for SUNRISE targets (`diffSeconds <= 0`, nearest), which is inside the dawn window. Shifting it further would have been inventing a preference with nothing to justify it.
- **`isWoodlandOnly` moved onto `LocationEntity`**, beside `supportsTargetType`, now that two callers need the same answer — the verdict fork in `BriefingSlotBuilder` and the event filter in `BriefingService`. It is a fact about the location, not about either caller.
- Applies only to **woodland-only** sites: a wood that also carries an open type (Allen Banks) keeps both events, because it has a horizon.

### Added — Woods are judged by woodland rules, not sky rules (V134)
- **Four sky rules fire on exactly the morning a woodland photographer sets an alarm for.** `low cloud > 80` → STANDDOWN *"Heavy cloud"*; `mid cloud ≥ 80` → STANDDOWN *"Overcast"*; `visibility < 5 km` → STANDDOWN *"Poor visibility"*; `humidity > 90` → flag *"Mist risk"*. Under a canopy those read: even light, the softbox you wanted, mist — the reason to be there — and mist *hoped for*, not risked. **This is an inverted polarity, not a threshold disagreement.**
- **New `WoodlandVerdictEvaluator`, a sibling of `BriefingVerdictEvaluator` rather than a branch inside it.** Putting woodland polarity in the class that defines sky polarity would mean every future verdict rule has to be reasoned about twice with opposite signs, in one place, forever. A second class costs one file and buys a single sign per file.
- **New `LocationType.WOODLAND`** — structural and year-round, orthogonal to `BLUEBELL`, which is a seasonal subject that happens to occur in woods. A site can be both (an enclosed bluebell wood), or either alone (Rannerdale Knotts is BLUEBELL on an open fell; a gorge is WOODLAND with no bluebells). Seeded from `bluebell_exposure = 'WOODLAND'` — an already-curated, twice-reviewed split (V84 seeded it, V109 corrected Roseberry).
- **The fork is per-location and deliberately narrow**: only a site whose *sole* subject is under canopy takes the woodland rules. A wood that also carries an open type — Allen Banks is a wood with an aspect — keeps the sky chain, because it has a horizon and the sky verdict is the one that decides whether to drive there.
- **Partly reverses V132, on purpose.** V132 said *"an enclosed wood has no horizon, so outside the bloom there is nothing for a sunrise forecast to be about"*. The first half stands; the second was too broad. A wood has plenty to forecast — mist, diffuse light, stillness — it simply is not what `BriefingVerdictEvaluator` measures. V132 removed woods from the **sky** product, correctly. This adds them to a **different** one.
- **The woodland branch returns before the tide lookup and the cloud-trend extraction** — a wood is never coastal, and its slot carries `TideInfo.NONE`.
- **Deliberately no rating and no model call.** The thresholds are photographic priors, not measurements: nothing here can score them yet, because `actual_outcome` has zero rows (verified against production). They are therefore set to be *cheap to be wrong about* — deterministic, free per run, revertible with one migration. A Claude-authored woodland subject is a separate, later decision that should wait for evidence.
- Both routing rules are mutation-verified: disabling the fork, or widening it to catch woods that also have an aspect, each turns exactly one test red.

### Fixed — "Last calculated" was unreadable once it aged past a day
- **Reported as "this last calculated field is rubbish to read with hours as the only unit".** Correct: `formatCalcTime` stopped at an unbounded hours tier, so a drive-time stamp left alone for a month read **"744h ago"** — a number nobody converts at a glance.
- **It was survivable for the briefing and not for this stamp.** The briefing regenerates every 8–10 hours, so its age rarely leaves the hours tier. Drive times only moved when a user pressed the button (until the nightly job in V133), so months were routine.
- **The logic existed twice** — `formatAge` in `DailyBriefing.jsx` and `formatCalcTime` in `UserSettingsModal.jsx` — and both terminated at hours. They had already drifted cosmetically: `"just now"` vs `"Just now"`, `"5m ago"` vs `"5 min ago"`. Now one `formatRelativeAge` in `utils/relativeTime.js`, with both wordings preserved behind a `verbose` flag rather than standardised away: each is pinned by a test and neither is wrong, and what mattered was that the **tiers** stop being duplicated.
- **Days are floored, not rounded** — 35 hours reads "1d ago", not "2d ago". An elapsed-time stamp must never claim more time has passed than actually has.
- **No weeks or months tier, deliberately.** "45d ago" needs no conversion; "1mo ago" quietly hides anything from 30 to 60 days, and this stamp exists precisely to show that something has gone stale.
- Mutation-verified: reverting to the unbounded-hours behaviour turns exactly the two days-tier tests red.

### Fixed — Bluebell scoring asked one precipitation reading to mean two different things

- **Found by auditing the other hot topics after the cloud-inversion fix**, for the same defect class. The hardcoding half was clean everywhere — inversion's literal `"strong"` was the only fact value in all 14 strategies that did not interpolate real data, and the other `intValue()` truncations are on metres and counts where nothing is lost. The *double-count* half was not.
- **`postRain` and `dryNow` could never both be true.** Both read the same instantaneous `precipitationMm` at the event slot, through `>= 0.5` and `< 0.2` — mutually unsatisfiable. But they are meant to describe *different times*: rain earlier freshens the flowers and ground cover, dry at the event makes it shootable. The combination the scoring most wants to reward was the one combination arithmetically impossible to express, however the weather actually behaved.
- **Three consequences, all now gone.** The nominal 10/10 was unreachable (real ceiling 9/10 for both exposures, so `Math.min(10, …)` was dead). `buildSummary`'s "post-rain freshness" clause was dead code in production — reachable only by a unit test calling the method directly with both flags set, which is how it stayed green. And the score dipped non-monotonically across the 0.2–0.5 mm gap where neither flag fired: holding mist + calm + soft light, bone dry scored 9, light drizzle 8, and steady rain 9 again — light drizzle scoring *below* heavy rain.
- **Fix: two readings instead of one.** `OpenMeteoResponseParser.extractPrecedingPrecipitationMm` sums the 6 hours *before* the event slot, excluding the event hour so the two readings stay independent, and rides `WeatherData.precedingPrecipitationMm`. `postRain` reads that window; `dryNow` still reads the event hour. No new API call — hourly `precipitation` was already fetched and already parsed for the event slot. Follows the existing `extractMistTrend` / `extractPressureTrend` pattern for windowed reads.
- **Fails soft.** With no window (Open-Meteo omitted precipitation, or the event is the first hour in the array) `postRain` falls back to the event-slot reading, degrading to the previous behaviour rather than silently withholding the bonus from every location.
- **Lower blast radius than the inversion bug, and worth saying so:** this 0–10 score is never displayed. It feeds the bluebell Claude prompt, and Claude returns the 1–5 that surfaces. So it misinformed the prompt rather than showing anyone a wrong number.
- **Audited and left alone, deliberately.** `AstroConditionsService` — cloud cover and surface visibility genuinely diverge (radiation fog under a clear sky; 40 km visibility under stratocumulus), it already rounds rather than truncates, and the fog hard-cap supersedes the visibility modifier rather than stacking with it. `ForecastStabilityClassifier` — its terms *do* correlate, since a front moves pressure, precipitation probability and weather codes together, but those are independent measurements that co-occur, not one quantity restated. Convergent evidence is the right design for a confidence score.
### Added — Drive times refresh nightly instead of only when someone presses the button (V133)
- **Reported as "why no driving distances for those two cards?"** Prod confirmed it: Hardwick Hall Country Park and Houghall Woods had **zero** `user_drive_time` rows while their neighbours had one each. The card was behaving correctly — it falls back to distance-only when no drive time is known, and a test pins that — so this was missing *data*, not a rendering bug.
- **Cause: drive times were only ever recalculated on an explicit user action.** `DriveDurationService.doRefreshForUser` covers `locationRepository.findAll()` with no filter, and `replaceForUser` swaps a user's whole set — so a location added *after* someone's last refresh simply had no row for them, indefinitely, with nothing to indicate why.
- **New `drive_time_refresh` scheduled job** at 02:40, after the nightly pipeline has settled and clear of the top-of-hour jobs. Registered with the DB-backed `DynamicSchedulerService`, so it can be paused, triggered or rescheduled from Manage → Operations → Scheduler like every other job. **The Settings button is unchanged** and remains the immediate override for someone who has just moved house.
- **No cooldown on the scheduled path, deliberately.** The manual path enforces a per-user cooldown so the button cannot be hammered; applying it here would make the job silently skip any user who pressed the button that evening — precisely the user whose home has most likely just changed.
- **The "last calculated" stamp only advances on a run that actually wrote rows.** A zero return (ORS unconfigured, rate-limited, or an empty response) is counted as a failure and leaves the previous stamp in place, so the UI keeps showing the last *real* refresh rather than claiming a fresh one over unchanged data.
- **One user's failure does not abandon the run** — each is refreshed independently, and a throw is logged and stepped over. Users with no saved home location are skipped: there is no origin to route from, so having no drive times is correct rather than stale.
- Both behaviours are mutation-verified: dropping the rows-written guard, or letting a failure propagate, each turns exactly one test red.

### Added — Cloud verification backfills itself hourly instead of needing a terminal session (V131)
- **The backlog cannot be drained in one pass.** ~25,000 evaluations at four archive sample points each is roughly 5,000 requests, far beyond Open-Meteo's hourly allowance. Observed in production: an unbounded run managed 13 batches (~1,250 evaluations) before the limit bit, stopped correctly — and then waited for a human to re-trigger it. Clearing the backlog that way meant sitting at a terminal re-POSTing for a day.
- **New `cloud_verification_backfill` scheduled job** takes a bounded slice each hour (12 batches ≈ 1,200 evaluations ≈ 240 requests) and yields. A full backlog clears in about a day unattended. Registered with the DB-backed `DynamicSchedulerService`, so it can be paused, triggered or rescheduled from Manage → Operations → Scheduler like every other job.
- Runs at **:20 past** — clear of the top-of-hour jobs, and a little after Open-Meteo's hourly allowance resets, so a tick throttled last hour starts with its quota back.
- **Cheap to leave enabled.** Once the backlog is clear the first batch finds no candidates and returns immediately. New evaluations become verifiable as they age past the ~6-day archive lag, so the job keeps the dataset current rather than needing a re-run after each forecast cycle.
- The manual `POST /backfill` is unchanged and still drains as far as it can; the scheduled tick defers to it if one is already running.

### Fixed — Bluebell woods were briefed as year-round sky locations, because a migration typed them wholesale
- **Reported as "why are Hardwick Hall Country Park and Houghall Woods in there at all — we're well out of bluebell season?"** They were there because `V84` gave all 16 new bluebell sites `LANDSCAPE` in a single blanket statement (`SELECT id, 'LANDSCAPE' FROM locations WHERE bluebell_exposure IS NOT NULL`), commented *"Each gets both LANDSCAPE and BLUEBELL"*. No per-site judgement was made. `BriefingService.isColourLocation` admits anything that is not `WILDLIFE`, so an enclosed wood with no horizon became a year-round sunrise candidate.
- **`bluebell_evaluate_year_round` was a workaround for that, and never worked.** `V111` added it to subtract what `V84` over-added — *"OQ4 from the Pass 3 bluebell extraction … The collector gate reads this flag once the bluebell mini-batch lands"*. **The gate was never built**, so the column has never had a reader: every row `TRUE`, absent from the admin UI, read by no code. **Dropped** rather than finally gated, because "is this wood worth an out-of-season trip" is already answered by whether the site also carries `LANDSCAPE`.
- **Three concepts, one home each, after this**: what a location *is* → `location_type`; *when* bluebells matter → the config-driven `SeasonalWindow`; and nothing else. `isColourLocation` now admits a location with a year-round colour type (`LANDSCAPE`/`SEASCAPE`/`WATERFALL`), **or** a `BLUEBELL` site while the bloom is on.
- **Candidacy is tested across the whole briefed window**, not just today, so a bloom opening in two days is not excluded from a briefing that already covers that day.
- **Twelve woods lose `LANDSCAPE`; five keep it.** Retained where the draw is a genuine open sightline with bluebells incidental — Roseberry Topping, Loughrigg Terrace, Rannerdale Knotts, Brandelhow Woods, Low Wood (Wasdale). Three are corroborated by this schema's own independent classification: `V84` seeded `bluebell_exposure = 'OPEN_FELL'` for Rannerdale and Loughrigg, and `V109` reclassified Roseberry — the same three the research rated highest-confidence, reached without sight of the column. **Allen Banks is deliberately untouched**: `V84` skipped it because it was a `LANDSCAPE` site before bluebell support existed, so its typing was considered rather than blanket.
- **Removing `LANDSCAPE` does not remove these sites from bluebell scoring or the bluebell hot topic** — `LocationRepository` selects them by `LocationType.BLUEBELL`.

### Fixed — The instant-paint cache could fail silently on the device it exists for
- **The two SWR caches are large and the budget is shared.** The briefing measures **1.28 MB** raw (~2.6 MB as UTF-16, ~52% of iOS Safari's ~5 MB `localStorage` ceiling) and `useForecasts` writes a second, modelled-larger payload alongside it. When the pair exceeds quota, `localStorage.setItem` throws — and `writeSwrCache` swallowed it into a bare `catch`.
- **The failure was structurally invisible.** A quota drop was indistinguishable from a first visit, a cold cache and a private-mode session. There is no telemetry anywhere in `frontend/src` (grep for sentry/posthog/analytics/reportError/navigator.storage returns nothing), so nobody could tell whether it was *already happening in production* — on mobile, in the field, which is exactly where instant paint earns its keep.
- **`writeSwrCache` now returns whether the write landed and warns when it does not**, naming the key, the size in KB and MB-as-UTF-16, and the error. Callers may still ignore it — caching stays best-effort — but a dropped write is now *representable* rather than erased.
- **Stale and corrupt entries are evicted on read.** `readSwrCache` treated them as a miss but left the bytes in place, so an expired 2.6 MB briefing went on occupying the space blocking the *other* cache's write — the worst of both: no instant paint from the stale copy, no room for the fresh one.
- **A failed write no longer leaves the previous generation behind.** The caller believes it just refreshed that key; serving the superseded copy next load is worse than a cold start, and it keeps starving the other cache.
- **A quota throw can no longer masquerade as an auth failure.** `axiosClient` persisted refreshed tokens inside the `.then()` that its own `.catch()` guards, so a storage failure would clear the tokens and fire `session-expired` — logging the user out at the moment the server had just issued them a valid token. The persist is now guarded separately; the refresh succeeded, and failing to cache it is not an auth failure.
- **Found a vacuous test while fixing this.** `write is a no-op when storage rejects` spied on `Storage.prototype.setItem`, but this environment's `localStorage` is a plain object carrying its own `setItem`, so the spy never intercepted and the rejection never happened. It now spies on the object and asserts the mock was actually called. Both new eviction behaviours are mutation-verified: reverting either turns exactly one test red.
- **Deliberately not done yet: choosing an eviction policy.** With the warning in place the failure is now observable, so the next step is to find out whether it is happening before deciding which cache wins a byte budget. Trimming the 7 days of *past* forecasts the map never reads (`ForecastController:133` starts the window at `today.minusDays(7)`) is the other candidate, and it is a behaviour change rather than a hardening.

### Changed — Measured the briefing payload, and the architecture doc's prediction was wrong
- **`plan-panel-data-contracts.md` called a payload trim "likely a bigger and cheaper win than restructuring contracts"**, on the strength of a research agent's ~770 KB estimate that the document itself flagged as unverified and told the reader to measure. Measured against production: the estimate was low, and the recommendation was wrong.
- **Ground truth**: `daily_briefing_cache` is **1,338,649 bytes raw** — 242 enabled locations across 7 regions, 1,936 slots at ~691 bytes each.
- **But gzip is on for `application/json`** (`application-prod.yml:162-165`) and the payload is highly repetitive, so the wire cost is **~133 KB**, under two main JS bundles, on a response that is ETag-revalidated to a 304 thereafter. Client cost measured in a browser: `JSON.parse` **5.2 ms**.
- **Only a quarter of the client cost is the payload.** Of 19.9 ms per fetch, ~15 ms is the SWR cache re-serialising and synchronously writing the object just parsed (`swrCache.js:41-46`). That is the cheap win, and it is not a payload-shape problem.
- **The real size finding is a quota one, not a bandwidth one**: at ~2.6 MB of UTF-16 the briefing alone takes ~52% of iOS Safari's ~5 MB `localStorage` budget before the (larger) forecasts cache is written. Tracked separately.
- **The server cost is real and stands**: every `GET /api/briefing` runs a DB query, a full walk *and reallocation* of all 1,936 slots, a second walk for the honesty filter, and ~1.3 MB of serialization — then `ShallowEtagHeaderFilter` buffers and hashes it. A shallow ETag saves bandwidth, never server work, so **a 304 discards all of it**. `HotTopicAggregator` adds 13 uncached `detect()` calls per request.
- **Recorded as "instrument before caching"**, with the sharp edges named (request-time London date feeds the confidence horizon; two of four `cache.set` sites publish no invalidation event; a region *rename* blanks the grid through name-keyed lookups) — and one explicit do-not: caching serialized bytes and hand-rolling the ETag would drop `/api/briefing` from `REVALIDATABLE_READ_PATHS`, silently reverting to `no-store` and killing the 304, a regression every existing test would pass.

### Fixed — The aurora overlay dropped `bestBetStatus`, silently disabling two features
- **Found while instrumenting the briefing serve path for performance work, not by a bug report** — which is the point. Nothing threw, nothing logged, and every existing test passed.
- **`getCachedBriefing` overlays live aurora and hot topics** onto the persisted briefing so simulation toggles take effect without a full refresh. When the live values differ it rebuilds the response — and did so through the **12-arg convenience constructor**, which delegates with `bestBetStatus = null`. It was the *only* production site that dropped it; `BriefingService:344`, `:490`, `:505`, `:515` and `BriefingHonestyFilter:116` all pass it explicitly.
- **Two consumers switch on that field, and both failed silently.** `applyBestBetFallback` returns early unless the status is `FAILED`, so the serve-time fallback never fired and the user saw the honest empty state instead of the last good pick. And `DailyBriefing.jsx:1300` renders the "from an earlier forecast" warning on `bestBetStatus === 'FAILED'`, so the staleness cue never appeared — under a comment reading *"Switch on the explicit bestBetStatus, not an inferred empty array"*, describing a guarantee the backend was not upholding.
- **Both went dark on exactly the requests that reach the rebuild branch** — whenever aurora is live or a hot-topic simulation is toggled, which is the moment the fallback exists for.
- **The existing tests missed it structurally**: their live aurora equals the cached value, so the overlay short-circuits at the `Objects.equals` early return and never rebuilds. The new test forces the rebuild with a differing live summary. **Verified by mutation** — reverting the one-line fix turns it red and nothing else in the 6,025-test suite notices.
- Also documents the trap on the 12-arg constructor: safe for building a response from scratch with no status yet, wrong for copying or overlaying an existing one.

## [v2.17.1] - 2026-07-28

### Changed — CHANGELOG's `[Unreleased]` split into the 13 releases it actually shipped in
- **`release.sh` tags but never touched `CHANGELOG.md`**, so every entry since v2.15.4 had accumulated under one `[Unreleased]` heading — 83 entries across 13 releases. The heading was false, and v2.17.0's release notes were indistinguishable from v2.16.2's.
- Attribution was mechanical rather than guesswork: each entry was added by exactly one commit, and each commit falls in exactly one tag range, so `git log -p -- CHANGELOG.md` plus `git rev-list <prev>..<tag>` maps entry → release. All 83 matched; none were ambiguous. Entry text is untouched — the 1,839 non-heading lines are identical before and after, verified as a set.

### Changed — `release.sh` refuses to tag with un-promoted release notes
- **`release.sh` tags but never touches `CHANGELOG.md`, so `[Unreleased]` drifted silently.** Between v2.15.4 and v2.17.0 that meant 83 entries across 13 releases piled under one heading, and untangling them afterwards needed a git-archaeology pass over the file's own history.
- **It now guards instead of drifting.** Two checks, run against the **target commit** rather than the working tree (the tag ships whatever `CHANGELOG.md` looks like at that commit): it refuses if `[Unreleased]` still holds entries, naming the count and the exact promotion steps; and it refuses if there is no `## [vX.Y.Z]` section for the version being tagged — which catches notes promoted under the wrong version number, the failure the first check alone would wave through.
- Deliberately a guard, not an action: `main` requires a PR, so the script cannot commit the promotion itself. Making the release *impossible* to cut with stale notes is the part that actually holds.

## [v2.17.0] - 2026-07-28

### Fixed — Cloud verification compared unlike things, then measured the difference as forecast error (V130)
- **Two flaws in the same comparison, found by running it.** Against 21,598 real comparisons the harness reported the forecast's solar-horizon low cloud as out by 33pp, biased 25pp toward "blocked". Both flaws had to be ruled out before that number could mean anything — and it turned out not to.
- **The baseline is offset, not the forecast.** ERA5 reads systematically *lower* low cloud than the forecast model: +24.67pp at the horizon and +14.88pp at the observer, a point the forecast is not extrapolating toward. Decisively, the bias is **flat across forecast horizons** (T+0 +25.19, T+1 +25.52, T+2 +21.01). Genuine forecast error must worsen with lead time; a model offset does not. So the report's absolute cuts (observed ≤20% "open", ≥60% "blocked") were mislabelling — a horizon truly at 60% reads nearer 40% and counted as open — and were **systematically biased toward finding the forecast at fault**.
- **The report now compares buckets against each other, not against thresholds.** Each bucket reports `meanForecastGapLow` / `meanObservedGapLow`, and the report adds two derived figures that are immune to the offset because it applies to every bucket equally: **`vetoSeparation`** (mean observed cloud when the veto fired, minus when it did not — positive means the veto discriminates) and **`capSeparation`** (uncapped minus capped — positive is the D7 signature).
- **The sampling was also not like-for-like.** Verification read a *single* centre-bearing point while the forecast's `solarLow` is a *3-point cone average*, so a sampling difference was being charged to forecast error. Verification now averages the same three bearings, at four archive points per evaluation instead of two.
- **V130 clears `cloud_verification`** — the horizon columns changed meaning, so old and new rows are not comparable and mixing them would bake an artefact into the very statistic the harness exists to produce. Derived data, rebuilt by one POST; it also clears the ~13.5% blank rows from the pre-#333 rate-limited run.

### Changed — Call the verification baseline what it is: reanalysis, not observation
- **The cloud-verification docs overstated their own evidence.** They described ERA5 as "the cloud that actually occurred" and "what actually happened". ERA5 is a *model reconstruction* that assimilates observations — genuinely independent of the forecast under test, since it uses data unavailable when that forecast was issued, but a model field nonetheless. Nobody measured "37% low cloud at this point at 21:00".
- **Why the distinction has teeth:** where ERA5 and the forecast model share a bias — low stratocumulus, marine layers, orographic cloud over the Pennines are the classic cases — the comparison *flatters* the forecast, and errors both make in the same direction are invisible. A disagreement should be read as "the forecast differs from a better-informed model", not "the forecast was wrong". It says nothing whatever about whether a sunset looked good; that still needs recorded outcomes.
- Corrected in `CLAUDE.md`, `OpenMeteoArchiveApi`, `CloudVerificationPair` and `AppConfig`. Also brings the `CLAUDE.md` entry up to date with #312 and #333, which it still contradicted: the backfill endpoint no longer takes `?limit=` (it is async), and "every attempt writes a row, so no retry storms" is now the opposite of the truth — blank rows are cleared and retried.

### Fixed — A throttled cloud-verification backfill silently blanked its own backlog
- **Open-Meteo signals a rate limit with `HTTP 200`** and the body `{"error": true, "reason": "Hourly API request limit exceeded."}`. Nothing about that is exceptional to the HTTP client, and it deserialised happily into a response whose `hourly` block was simply null — which reads downstream as *"the archive has no data for this point"*, not *"we were throttled"*.
- **The consequence was the worst available.** Verification rows were written carrying no observations; because candidate selection is an anti-join, every one of those rows then counted as verified **forever** and was never revisited. A throttled run would work through its entire remaining backlog writing permanent blanks, with no exception, no warning, and a clean-looking finish reporting `lastError: null`. Observed in production against a live backfill.
- **Three fixes**, each closing a different link in that chain: the archive client now rejects the error envelope instead of parsing it as empty data; `backfill` reports rows-written *and* rows-with-observations separately (`BackfillBatch`), so "wrote 100 blanks" is distinguishable from "backlog drained"; and the runner stops on a blank batch, recording it in `lastError`, rather than grinding on.
- **Self-healing.** Blank rows are cleared at the start of each run, so a throttled backfill repairs itself on the next POST rather than needing manual SQL. The stop-on-blank rule is what stops that from becoming a retry loop against a sustained outage.

### Added — "Close to home": the Plan tab's low-risk *local* decision, plus the journey reading order

- **The missing stage of the user's journey.** The Plan tab already answered the glance ("anything worth a trip?") and the drill-in ("show me on the map"), but nothing answered the step between them: *what is within easy reach* — the low-risk local option that tips you out the door without gambling a long drive. New `CloseToHome` block, mounted between the headline picks and the hot topics.
- **Proximity gates; region never does — and the boundary bug this avoids is real.** An earlier draft pinned the user's *home region*. St Mary's Lighthouse, one of the strongest locations in the North East, sits in southern Northumberland — so a Tyne & Wear user would have had their best nearby spot filtered out by their own home region. Candidates are gated on straight-line distance from the saved home postcode (`RADIUS_MILES = 22`) and cross region lines freely; a location's region is *displayed* as an honesty label but never filtered on. This also means **no "home region" setting**, and none should be added.
- **Distance gates, rating sorts.** Distance appears on every card, so it carries no weight in the ordering and no tiebreak — in the reference data the closest spot (Tynemouth Priory, 7 mi) ranks third, which is the rule working. Ties between equal integer ratings break on soonest, then name: both neutral with respect to distance.
- **Two parts, no modes, not scoped to the selected day.** Part 1 is a breadcrumb for the very next solar event — verdict, one plain sentence of *why*, and the next local window worth leaving the house for on a gold rail. Part 2 is the top four rated spots within reach across the 3-day horizon. **Part 1 is never hidden on a poor verdict**: an honest "stay in" plus a date to look forward to beats an absent block. Part 2 hides alone when nothing qualifies, and a stand-down spot never reaches a card — an earlier iteration named the best nearby location at 1.8★ on a bad night, which undercut the stay-in verdict.
- **The day chip is conditional.** Shown only when the result set actually spans more than one day; when all four land on the same day it is pure repetition, and the day is already carried by the next-window column.
- **No new backend, no new endpoint, no new setting.** Distance reuses the postcode → coordinates pipeline that already backs the `🚗 NN min` drive chips (`GET /api/user/settings`); ratings come from the briefing payload the tab already fetches, preferring the cached-evaluation scores exactly as `HeatmapGrid` does. With no postcode saved the block renders nothing, as it does when nothing at all sits inside the radius — a permanently empty block is noise, not honesty.
- **Region and travel labels sit at `--ink-2`, deliberately.** At 10.5px, `--ink-3` measures 3.55:1 on the plain card and 3.39:1 on the gold lead card, failing AA's 4.5:1; `--ink-2` lands at ~6.9:1 on both. The region line *is* the "St Mary's Lighthouse / Northumberland" honesty label — it has to be readable. Do not lower these.
- **Reading order now follows the journey**: picks → **close to home** → hot topics → briefing summary → full grid. Decisions before explanations. Only the new block moved; sections 1, 3, 4 and 5 are untouched.
- **Location-focused map drill-down reuses the existing overlay** rather than building a second modal — a card hands `(date, targetType, locationName)` to the same seam every other location affordance uses, so `buildMapOverlay`'s `location` trigger flies to that single pin and auto-opens its popup, with siblings dimmed for context.
- **View switcher is now a segmented control** — a bordered track with a raised active segment, replacing the underlined text row that read as three floating labels with no affordance that it was a switch. The leading glyphs (`◉ ◍ ⚙`) are `aria-hidden`, so each segment's accessible name stays the plain word.
- **Drill-down emphasis is expressed in the app's own marker language.** The overlay opens focused on one location, so the target pin scales up and its neighbours mute, keyed on a new overlay-only `emphasiseLocationName` prop — the Map tab keeps every pin equal. The design mock drew flat coloured circles for this; building those literally would have given a 4★ pin one appearance on the Map tab and another in the overlay, so the existing rating-ramp marker is scaled and dimmed instead. Emphasis is part of the `DivIcon` cache key, so the Map tab can never be served a muted icon.
- **Four defects found by an adversarial review pass and fixed before merge**, each now pinned by a test that fails when the fix is reverted:
  - **"Next local window" named whichever spot the sun reached first, not the best one.** The sort key embedded each slot's own `solarEventTime`, which is computed per location from its lat/lon — so two spots at the *same* sunrise differ by minutes and never tie, making the intended rating tiebreak unreachable. The block would point at a 3★ spot directly above a lead card showing 5★ for the same event. Keyed on the solar **event** now; three minutes is not a different window.
  - **The event time could render blank.** It was read from the first in-radius candidate, which is absent when every nearby location is configured for the other solar event, or when the honesty filter clears a zero-coverage region. Falls back to the event summary's own time — off by minutes for a distant location, which beats absent.
  - **The card hover border was dead CSS.** The component set `border` in the inline `style`, and a style-attribute declaration outranks any class rule without `!important`, so the specified hover tint silently never applied — while a comment above the rule asserted the opposite. Border and background moved into `.cth-card`, keyed on the `data-lead` attribute the component already sets.
  - **The focus ring failed WCAG 2.2 SC 1.4.11.** `--plex-border-light` measures **1.58:1** on this surface against the 3:1 required for a non-text indicator, and because it replaced the UA ring, keyboard users were worse off than with no rule at all. Now the block's gold at **7.14:1**, measured in a browser.
- Frontend suite green at 1819 → 1869 tests; **50 new** — 31 in `closeToHome.test.js` (the selection rules, pinned as rules: distance never sorts, region never filters, a stand-down never reaches a card, soonest beats better-rated *across* events while better-rated wins *within* one), 15 in `CloseToHome.test.jsx`, 3 in `DailyBriefing.test.jsx` for the reading order, and 1 guarding the switcher's accessible name.

## [v2.16.10] - 2026-07-27

### Fixed — Every cloud inversion read "9/10 · strong", because nothing in the pipeline measured an inversion

- **Reported as "it's always 9/10 — is that hardcoded?". It was not hardcoded, but three gates stacked so that no other value could reach the screen.** The word "strong" was a string literal in `InversionHotTopicStrategy` (`topScore + "/10 · strong"`), so the fact line could not disagree with itself. The detector only admits scores ≥ 9, so 9 and 10 were the only numbers that could ever reach it. And in the STRONG band the calculator could only produce 9.0, 9.5 or 10.0, while `PromptBuilder` reported it through `Double.intValue()` — truncation, so 9.0 and 9.5 both read as 9 and only an exact 10.0 could read as 10. Two of the three reachable values collapsed onto the third.
- **The score counted moisture twice, so an ordinary calm dawn scored 9 before cloud was considered.** `InversionScoreCalculator` awarded 4 points for the temperature–dew point gap and 2 more for relative humidity, but RH *is* a function of the gap. Checked with Magnus across −5 °C to 25 °C: a gap ≤ 1 °C implies RH 92.7–94.2 %, which always earned the full humidity bonus. Six of ten points came from one reading. Add the 3 points for light wind — the same synoptic setup that produces the small gap, since both come from nocturnal radiative cooling under a high — and any calm, saturated UK autumn morning totalled 9 with no cloud contribution at all. The humidity term is gone; the scale is now moisture 0–5, wind 0–3, cloud deck 0–2.
- **Low cloud was worth one point, so two contradictory skies both read STRONG.** 100 % low cloud scored 9.5 (a viewpoint sitting *inside* the murk) and 0 % scored 9.0 (a "sea of clouds" with no cloud in it). A test asserted the first as correct. Low cloud outside 15–80 % now caps the score at 6.0, below the MODERATE band, so neither can produce a prompt block or a hot topic.
- **Nothing measured a temperature inversion.** An inversion is temperature *increasing* with height; every input was a surface reading. `OpenMeteoClient.FORECAST_PARAMS` now requests `temperature_925hPa` and `temperature_850hPa`, and the calculator requires a measured reversal against `temperature_2m`: no reversal caps at 6.0, a reversal weaker than +2 °C caps at 8.0, and only a well-defined one can reach STRONG. 925 hPa (≈ 760 m, the scale of a UK valley inversion) is preferred; 850 hPa is a stricter fallback.
- **Missing profile data caps at MODERATE rather than failing either way.** Claiming STRONG with no vertical profile is what produced the original complaint; dropping to zero would silently retire the feature. `ForecastDataAugmentor` logs a WARN when an inversion-eligible location arrives without one, so an empty strip is distinguishable from a spell of poor conditions rather than something to infer.
- **Elevation is now used quantitatively, not just as an on/off gate.** It was checked only against the 200 m eligibility threshold. A stable layer deeper than the viewpoint means the photographer is inside it, so the score is capped at 6.0 when `elevation <= boundary_layer_height` — data already fetched and, until now, unused by this feature. The comparison is approximate (Open-Meteo reports layer height above the grid cell's ground, elevation is above sea level) and the Javadoc says so; it is directionally right for the case that matters, since a nocturnal stable layer collapses to tens of metres while a daytime mixed layer runs past a kilometre.
- **Claude was being handed the answer and asked for it back.** The prompt stated `Score: 9/10 (Strong Cloud Inversion Potential)` and the output schema asked for `inversion_score`, whose value `ForecastScoreWriter` then stored — so the "Claude-evaluated" score was the calculator's number laundered through the model, which is a second reason it never varied. The block now also states the **measured reversal** in °C, the one piece of evidence that cannot be read off the score, and both the block and the system guidance tell Claude to weigh it over the score when they disagree. The guidance previously said `STRONG (9-10) → set rating 5` with nothing able to override it; a negative reversal now explicitly suppresses that boost.
- **The hot-topic band comes from the row.** `SurvivorSignals.Scores` gained `inversionBand`, carried on the INVERSION row's `summary` column where `ForecastScoreWriter` already wrote the NONE/MODERATE/STRONG classification. Legacy rows have no band and fall back to "strong" — honest, since the detector only admits that band — but a mislabelled row is now visible instead of being painted over.
- **Two sweeps over the whole input space pin the invariants** rather than trusting the boundary cases: no cloud field outside 15–80 % can score STRONG, and **no combination of surface readings whatsoever can score STRONG without a measured reversal**. That second one is the direct guard against the original complaint. `PromptGoldenMasterTest` fixtures regenerated for the two prompt changes.
- **No migration.** The 0–10 scale, the 7/9 band boundaries and both database columns are unchanged; only what produces the number changed.

### Added — T5: outbound contract tests and Claude parse resilience (Phase 2 of the test strategy)
- **77 tests, all green, ~5.8s marginal on the PR gate.** No Docker, no Spring context, no network. Targets the joint-largest escaped-defect category: 8 defects, every one caught in production rather than by a test.
- **The gap this closes was documented in the codebase's own words.** `RestClientMocks`'s Javadoc: *"Because `uri(...)` is matched with `any(...)`, these helpers do NOT exercise URL-building."* Every client test used those helpers, so **no test in this repo asserted an outbound URL** — a wrong path, parameter name or unit stayed green.
- **The thesis was proven, not asserted.** The `92cb95b5` defect was temporarily reintroduced into main source (`OpenMeteoForecastApi:32`, `wind_speed_unit` → `wind_speed_units`, the typo that displayed 77 mph instead of ~21): the new `OpenMeteoForecastApiContractTest` went **red**; the existing `OpenMeteoClientTest` stayed **green, 18/18**. The mutation was reverted.
- **Ten contract classes** drive the real `HttpServiceProxyFactory` + `RestClientAdapter` over `MockRestServiceServer`, so `@GetExchange`/`@RequestParam` binding genuinely executes. They pin the exact path, the snake_case parameter names **with the wrong spelling asserted absent** (a presence-only assertion passes if both are sent), asymmetric coordinates `54.7753 / -1.5849` so a lat/lon transposition cannot slip through, and `arg0` asserted absent — which turns loss of the `-parameters` compiler flag into a hard failure instead of a silent 400. The 414 guard (`b534cf31`) is driven at the shape that caused it: 200 full-precision coordinates, asserting exactly `ceil(200/BATCH_COORD_LIMIT)` requests each under 8192 bytes.
- **`AnthropicWireMockFixtureContractTest`** asserts every WireMock fixture still deserialises into the Anthropic SDK's own types, enumerating them **reflectively** so fixtures added later stay covered, and asserting the discovered set is non-empty so it cannot pass vacuously. It encodes the `1d838df5` mis-nested error envelope permanently rather than checking it once by hand.
- **`ClaudeResponseParseResilienceTest`** drives nine `claude-malformed/` fixtures through the production parse path. Each asserts the parser does not throw, salvages what it can, and records a disposition — the third assertion being the whole lesson of `8fb3ad26` (*"because empty picks are indistinguishable from an honest decline, the drop hid for weeks"*).

### Found — Four gaps the parse-resilience tests exposed, all pinned by passing tests rather than fixed
- **No production code calls `RatingValidator.validateScore`** (`service/evaluation/RatingValidator.java:74`; only `validateRating` has call sites). A response carrying `fiery_sky: 491` and `golden_hour: -3` is **persisted verbatim** and logged `succeeded=true, error_type=null`. The request-side JSON schema does not save this either: `PromptBuilder` constrains `rating` with `enum [1..5]` but types the two scores as bare `integer`, with the bound only in a free-text description.
- **Truncation at `max_tokens` is invisible on the forecast batch path.** `stopReason` is read only by `BriefingBestBetAdvisor` — the `dc770582` lesson was applied to the best-bet advisor alone. Cut mid-`summary`, the slot fails outright (`error_type='parse_error'`); cut *after* `summary` closes, **the regex fallback silently rescues it, drops the headline, and writes `succeeded=true`**. Neither path ever says "truncated". `api_call_log.stop_reason`, populated in `BatchResultProcessor`, would carry the signal.
