- **The `8fb3ad26` distinction is still unrepresentable at the product surface.** A slot whose response failed to parse produces no `cached_evaluation` entry, and its *absence* is byte-identical to never-submitted, eligibility-skipped or triaged; `forecast_run_disposition` does not close it (`DispositionCategory` is collection-time only, and the slot still reads `EVALUATED`). Worse, the `api_call_log` write is **skipped entirely when `ResultContext.jobRunId()` is null**, so on that path a lost, paid-for evaluation is recorded nowhere but a WARN line. Note `JobRunService.logBatchResult` already **accepts a `status` parameter (line 220) and silently discards it** — `ApiCallLogEntity` has no such field, so PARSE_FAILED / MALFORMED_ID / NO_MESSAGE / NO_TEXT / LOCATION_NOT_FOUND all collapse into four `error_type` values plus free text.
- **An honest decline is discriminated from a genuine 1★ night by a prose sentence only** — same rating, same null `triageReason`, and a clean `succeeded=true, error_type=null` row.
- **A defect in the test double itself:** `AnthropicWireMockFixtures.succeededWithEvaluation:167` escapes `"` but not `\` when interpolating a summary, so a summary containing a backslash yields invalid JSON. Left unfixed here deliberately — it is a shared fixture file and wants its own change.
- Also corrected a recon error, now pinned: `parse_error` rows written by `ForecastResultHandler` **do** carry `targetDate`/`targetType` (decoded from the custom id) and are joinable to their slot; only `BatchResultProcessor`'s inline failures pass `(null, null)`.

### Fixed — CI failed at random because the Postgres testcontainer moved out from under a cached Spring context

- **What broke.** `Deploy` for `v2.16.9` failed in its `Run CI / Backend` job, so no image was built or pushed and production was never touched. Three `DispositionWriteIntegrationTest` tests errored with `CannotCreateTransaction … Connection to localhost:32774 refused`, each after burning a 60 s Hikari timeout. The same three failed on the two preceding pushes to `main` and passed on the one in between, with no relevant code change in any of them — the tell that this was scheduling, not a regression.
- **Cause: `@Testcontainers` + `@Container` on a `static` field.** That combination hands the container's lifecycle to JUnit's `TestcontainersExtension`, which stops a shared container in `afterAll` of *every* test class and restarts it in the next class's `beforeAll` — on a **new random host port**. Spring's context cache does not restart with it. `@DynamicPropertySource` is evaluated only when a context is *created*, so a later class with the same context cache key is handed the cached context, still holding the previous port in `spring.datasource.url`, and every query dies at a port nothing is listening on.
- **Which classes collide is luck, which is why it looked flaky.** Under `forkCount=1C` the failure needs two classes that *share* a context cache key to land in the same surefire fork. `IntegrationTestBaseSmokeTest`, `DispositionWriteIntegrationTest` and `ForecastBatchPipelineIntegrationTest` share one — they declare nothing context-mutating. The two `@MockitoBean` classes and `HttpIntegrationTestBaseProbeTest` (`@AutoConfigureMockMvc`) each have their own, and a distinct key forces a fresh context boot, which re-reads the new port and silently papers over the bug. So the classes that kept passing were passing *by accident*, on the same mechanism that killed the others.
- **Why it surfaced now.** #312 added the T4 tier, taking the Testcontainers classes from five to seven and repacking the forks. The hazard had been latent since June; the seventh class made it likely enough to hit three times in one morning.
- **Fix: `@DirtiesContext(classMode = AFTER_CLASS)` on `IntegrationTestBase`.** No context now outlives the class that created it, so none can outlive the container restart that happens between classes, and the next class re-reads the new port. One annotation, no change to what any test asserts. It costs at most two extra Spring context boots per full run — and only in the packings that were previously *broken*, since two same-key classes in one fork could never both work anyway.
- **The singleton-container pattern was tried first and rejected on evidence, not taste.** Dropping `@Testcontainers`/`@Container` for a static initialiser gives one container on one stable port and removes the restart and the redundant Flyway run per class — strictly the tidier design. It also broke seven tests. The per-class restart turns out to be load-bearing: it is what hands each class a virgin, freshly-migrated database. Several subclasses call `regionRepository.deleteAll()` in `@AfterEach`, which deletes the rows **Flyway seeded** (V31's five regions), not merely the rows they created; with one long-lived container those deletions leak across classes and `HttpIntegrationTestBaseProbeTest` fails on an empty `regions` table. Making the singleton work means first teaching every subclass not to delete seed data — a bigger change than this bug warrants, and not one to make with CI red. Both the reasoning and the rejected alternative are recorded in the base class Javadoc so the next person does not re-derive it.
- **Reproduced before it was fixed, on the exact commit CI failed on.** Forcing `forkCount` to `1` puts all seven classes in one JVM — the guaranteed-worst packing — and produced **8 errors** across the two later key-sharing classes while the other four passed. The failing two were identifiable at a glance as the only classes with no `Started … in Ns` line, i.e. the only ones handed a cached context. Worth knowing for the next repro: `-DforkCount=1` on the command line does nothing here, because `backend/pom.xml` sets `<forkCount>1C</forkCount>` explicitly and explicit plugin configuration beats a CLI user property — the pom has to be edited. A first attempt that passed `-DforkCount=1` silently ran eight forks, gave every class its own JVM, and "passed" while proving nothing.

### Removed — H2-era production leftovers, one of which could start an empty database
- **Deleted `backend/docker-compose.yml`.** It was referenced by no workflow, script or document — `deploy.yml`, `rollback.sh` and `migrate-pgdata-path.sh` all operate on the *root* compose file after `cd ~/goldenhour` — but it was not harmless. It declared `SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL: jdbc:h2:file:/data/goldenhour`, a `/Users/gregochr/goldenhour-data` bind mount that has not existed since #310 moved production data to `/home`, and `container_name: goldenhour-backend` — **the same name as the production container**. Anyone running `docker compose -f backend/docker-compose.yml up` would have collided with production and pointed the app at an H2 file that is not there; Spring does not fail on that, it starts an empty database and serves it. That is the same silent-empty-database failure mode `deploy.yml`'s PGDATA guard exists to prevent, left sitting in the tree with a loaded name.
- **Deleted `scripts/migrate-h2-to-postgres.sh`.** The migration it performs is done and unrepeatable; nothing references it. `scripts/migrate-pgdata-path.sh` (the `/Users` → `/home` move) is unrelated and stays.
- **Corrected three stale README claims**, each verified against the tree rather than assumed: the tech table said the database was "H2 file database + Flyway migrations (V1-V67)" when production is Postgres 17 and the highest migration is `V129`; the Docker section said production persisted "H2 to the Mac filesystem"; and `solar-utils` was listed as "v1.2.0 (GitHub Packages)" when `backend/pom.xml` resolves `2.1.0` from JitPack (`https://jitpack.io`).
- **Corrected a fourth claim found while checking the third.** The README said production Postgres lives "in a named volume that persists across container restarts". It does not: `docker-compose.yml` bind-mounts `/home/gregochr/goldenhour-data/postgres` and declares no top-level `volumes:` block at all. That is a backup-relevant error — it would send someone to `docker volume ls` for a database that is a plain directory on the host.
- **H2 is not being retired from the project**, only from production. `application-local.yml` and `src/test/resources/application.yml` still use it, deliberately: local dev needs no Docker, and the test suite runs in-memory. The README and DEVOPS.md sections describing the local H2 console are correct and untouched.

## [v2.16.9] - 2026-07-27

### Fixed — The required CodeQL check ran on the production host, where it could block every merge
- **`codeql.yml` moved from `self-hosted` to `ubuntu-latest`.** `CodeQL Analysis` is one of three required status checks on `main`, and the self-hosted runner *is* the production box. When that box is unwell — powered down, Docker wedged, runner service not restarted after a reboot — a job targeting it does not fail, it **queues**. A required check stuck in `queued` blocks merge without ever reporting, and with one admin and zero required reviewers there is nothing to clear it.
- **This is the failure mode the other required checks do not have.** A red Backend or Frontend check is answerable with a commit; "the runner is offline" is answerable with no commit at all. It also tied the ability to merge *any* change to the health of the machine serving production — the coupling you least want on the day production is broken.
- **Nothing in the job needed that box**: checkout, `setup-java`, CodeQL init, `mvnw compile -DskipTests`, analyze. No Docker, no production database, no local paths. `solar-utils` resolves from **JitPack** (`backend/pom.xml` declares `https://jitpack.io`; CLAUDE.md's "GitHub Packages" note is stale), so no `~/.m2/settings.xml` token is involved, and `ci.yml` already runs the strictly larger `./mvnw clean verify` on `ubuntu-latest`. The repo is public, so hosted minutes are free.
- **Added `timeout-minutes: 30`** — roughly 3× the observed median — so a hung analysis becomes a failure a commit can respond to rather than a check that never reports.
- **Measured, not assumed:** this job was *already* the PR gate's critical path at 8m35s–11m30s across seven runs, against the backend job's ~7m. The gate is CodeQL-bound either way. A hosted runner may be slower still; the PR making this change is the measurement.
- **Context.** This was the real obstacle to enabling `enforce_admins`, not `npm audit`. An earlier attempt to move `npm audit` off the required gate was **abandoned on the evidence**: the audit is self-clearing inside the PR that trips it (commit `e3d8bc64` is the record of exactly that, not of a lockout), it has resolved 8 of 8 advisory episodes in 4.5 months with a fix available every time, and removing it would have left Dependabot's auto-merged npm bumps and the `v*` release path with nothing reading the lockfile at all. Scoping it to `--omit=dev` was rejected for the same reason: `npm ls brace-expansion --omit=dev` is empty, so the only advisory on record lives entirely in the dev tree, where 577 of 654 dependencies are.

### Fixed — Two role-gating claims in CLAUDE.md that the code never enforced
- **Documentation only. No controller, `SecurityConfig` or test changed** — both divergences were resolved as *the doc is wrong*, deliberately and after checking the intent, rather than by tightening the code to match prose nobody had verified.
- **`GET /api/tides*` was documented under "Tides (ADMIN)"**, but `TideController` carries no `@PreAuthorize` and `SecurityConfig` gates `/api/**` at `.authenticated()` only, so any authenticated user could always read it. Bearer is the intent: tide extremes are astronomical data already surfaced in the forecast UI, and an ADMIN gate would break the app for every non-admin. Corrected, and the undocumented `GET /api/tides/stats/all` added.
- **The roles table called `LITE_USER` "read-only forecast and outcomes"** while `POST /api/outcome` is reachable by any authenticated user. Left open on purpose: `actual_outcome` holds zero rows and observations are the scarce input to the calibration gate — the only non-self-referential accuracy measure in the project — so restricting contributors works against the table's purpose. "Read-only" now scopes explicitly to the forecast surface.
- Surfaced by the integration test strategy work (#311), which flagged both as decisions rather than test tasks. Recorded here so the next reader finds the decision instead of re-deriving it — and because a test written against the old wording would have cemented behaviour the code never had.

### Changed — Test-gate hygiene: Phase 1 of the integration test strategy
- **The duplicate FindSecBugs step is gone from `ci.yml`, worth ~34s a run.** `clean verify` had already run the identical analysis: `spotbugs-maven-plugin`'s `spotbugs-check` execution is bound to the `verify` phase, and `findsecbugs-plugin:1.14.0` sits in that plugin's **plugin-level** `<configuration>/<plugins>`, which Maven applies to every invocation of every goal — lifecycle-bound or CLI. Verified empirically rather than by reading the pom: the emitted `target/spotbugsXml.xml` carries `<Plugin id='com.h3xstream.findsecbugs' enabled='true'/>` alongside detector timings for `TaintDataflowEngine`, `PathTraversalDetector`, `CrlfLogInjectionDetector` and ~25 more. The standalone step passed no flag, profile or config file the bound execution lacks, and analysed strictly less (`compile` rather than `verify`). Security coverage is unchanged; the comment left in its place says so, and says to change the pom rather than this file if you want it stricter.
- **`real-api-smoke.yml` now reports, and distinguishes the two ways it can tell you nothing.** It was the last workflow without a failure notifier. A hard failure — the batch returned and the parse assertions failed — opens the standard issue. An **inconclusive** run gets its own job and its own issue title, because the test aborts via `Assumptions.assumeTrue`: JUnit records a *skip*, the job stays green, and `failure()` can never reach it. The batch SLA is up to 24h against a 4h test budget, so consecutive inconclusives are plausible and mean the completion path has verified nothing for days while the tick stayed green. The separate title keeps "Real API Smoke is failing" meaning the parse broke, never that Anthropic was slow; the notifier's dedupe-into-comments *is* the streak counter, since a prior inconclusive has conclusion `success` and is indistinguishable from a verified pass through the runs API.
- **`PitExclusionDriftTest` ratchets the exclusion list that four red Mondays were caused by.** It walks `src/test/java`, finds every `@Tag("prompt-regression")` class, and fails if one is missing from PIT's `<excludedTestClasses>` — scoped to the text *inside* that element rather than a `contains` over the whole pom, so a class name appearing in a nearby comment cannot satisfy it. It also asserts the tagged set is non-empty, because a walk that silently found nothing would make the guard vacuous, which is the exact failure mode this work exists to remove.
- **Three classes lost their blanket `@MockitoSettings(strictness = LENIENT)`**, and the unnecessary stubs it had been hiding were deleted rather than converted to per-stub `lenient()`. Class-wide leniency disables `UnnecessaryStubbingException` for every test in the file, which is how a test keeps passing after the code it stubs stops being called.
- **`MacOsToastNotificationServiceTest` lost two tests that could not fail and gained one that can.** On a Linux runner `/usr/bin/osascript` does not exist, so the service swallows the failure and `assertThatNoException` is guaranteed regardless of correctness. The two purely vacuous cases are deleted; the quote case was promoted into a real assertion that an embedded quote cannot terminate the AppleScript literal — the property the recent backslash-ordering fix actually turns on.
- Deleted `LocationFailureTrackingUIDemo.java` (no `@Test`, referenced only by PIT's exclude list, which goes with it) and `frontend/src/api/healthApi.js` (nothing imports it).
- **`HttpIntegrationTestBase` and one provisional probe are landed deliberately unfinished.** The strategy's full-stack acceptance tier is budgeted at +45s on the PR gate and that is the number the plan trusts least, because a new base class adds a second Spring context cache key under `forkCount=1C`. Phase 5 is 26 developer-hours and is scoped off it. **The measurement is now in**, taken from CI run 30244459280 on `ubuntu-latest` (the dev Mac has no Docker daemon, so it could not be taken locally). The probe class cost **34.99 s** inside its own fork — that is the second context boot, in full. But the backend job's wall clock went **6m56s → 6m59s**: the ~35 s is almost entirely absorbed by `forkCount=1C` parallelism and offset by the 34 s the FindSecBugs deletion gave back. Two caveats before Phase 5 leans on this. The comparison is confounded — the same commit added the backfill feature and its two test classes — and it is one class, not the five T4 plans, so it says nothing about what happens when the forks saturate. What it does establish: the gate is at 6m59s against the plan's 7m30s hard ceiling, and a second Spring context cache key is not the wall-clock disaster the +45 s estimate feared.
- **Documented the Docker prerequisite**, which nothing said before: `./mvnw clean verify` starts a `postgres:17-alpine` testcontainer for five classes, so a developer with Docker stopped gets an opaque Testcontainers stack trace and no explanation. Also corrected stale docs — the SpotBugs threshold is `High`, not medium, and the macOS-shaped production paths that PR #310 moved.
- **Removed a CHANGELOG entry for a fix that never happened.** An entry claiming `SkyRatingEvalTest`'s PIT `<param>` had been "pasted into the explanatory comment" was false: it has always been inside `<excludedTestClasses>` (the line above merely names the class in prose). It recorded an agent's own temporary negative-control edit as a production defect. Deleted rather than reworded — the guard needs no origin story it did not have.
- **Found while doing this, and it changes Phase 5:** nothing in this repo runs a class named `*IT`. There is no failsafe plugin and no surefire `<includes>` override, so surefire's defaults apply (`*Test`, `Test*`, `*Tests`, `*TestCase`). Six planned Phase 5 classes carry the `*IT` suffix and would compile, look like tests, and never execute.

### Changed — Cloud-verification backfill runs in the background
- **A synchronous backfill cannot finish inside a request.** The backlog is ~24,600 evaluations; `POST /backfill?limit=500` was killed by the reverse proxy (returning an unparseable gateway page) while the work carried on server-side and committed anyway — the worst kind of feedback, since a timeout is indistinguishable from a failure.
- **`POST /api/admin/cloud-verification/backfill` now returns `202` immediately** and works the whole backlog in the background, 100 evaluations per committed batch. Poll **`GET /api/admin/cloud-verification/backfill/status`** for `running` / `verified` / `batches` / `remaining` / `lastError`. No shell loop needed — one POST drains the queue.
- **`409 Conflict` on a concurrent start.** Two runs would select overlapping candidates and race to insert the same `forecast_evaluation_id`, which the unique constraint rejects — turning a duplicate request into a batch of failures.
- **The runner is deliberately a separate bean** from `CloudVerificationService`. Calling `backfill(...)` from inside that service is a self-invocation and bypasses its own proxies, so neither `@Async` nor `@Transactional` would apply — the run would block the request thread and each batch would lose its transaction. Crossing a bean boundary keeps both.
- Stops at the first failing batch rather than grinding on: the usual cause is the archive rate-limiting, and continuing would write thousands of rows with no observations that the anti-join would then never revisit. Each batch commits independently, so a restart mid-run resumes rather than repeating.

### Added — Integration & UI test strategy, designed against the escaped-defect record
- **`docs/engineering/integration-test-strategy.md`.** Three strategies were drafted from competing philosophies (constraint-maximalist, ROI-pragmatist, production-first) and each was attacked by two adversarial reviewers before synthesis. Planning document only — no test, workflow or build change ships with it.
- **The evidence base is ~37 defects that reached production**, recovered from 505 `fix:` commits and the investigation docs. The distribution is what drives every decision in the plan: 8 cross-component wiring, 8 external-contract drift, 6 cache coherence, 6 product-semantic honesty, 5 CI self-failure, 4 scheduler silent death. Almost none was a wrong computation — the class 247 unit test classes and an 80% JaCoCo gate already cover. The recurring signature is **silence**: no exception, no log line, and detection by a human running SQL against production rather than by any test.
- **So the target shape is a T rather than a triangle**, and the reasoning is deliberately recorded because it is counter-intuitive: the two tiers that catch this defect class *without* someone first anticipating the bug are static rules (they constrain code not yet written) and production data probes (they observe what actually happened). A diamond was rejected for the same reason — it is still hand-authored assertions, so it inherits the blind spot that produced the defects.
- **Three claims common to all three drafts were false against the tree and are corrected inline.** `scripts/rollback.sh` and `scripts/pre-release-backup.sh` exist and are wired into `deploy.yml` (what is missing is anything that *decides* to invoke rollback); `GET /api/status` does not exist, as `StatusController` declares only `/stream`, and two drafts built deploy verification on it; MSW is inert as a frontend contract mechanism because 40 of 82 test files `vi.mock` the api module itself, so no request is ever issued.
- **Existing infrastructure is further along than a fresh reading suggests**, which narrows the plan considerably: the five `IntegrationTestBase` classes already run Testcontainers Postgres 17 + Flyway + WireMock on every PR (they match surefire's default includes with no tag or guard), and 93 `isForbidden()` assertions already execute through the real filter chain with no `addFilters = false` anywhere.
- **Splitting those classes to maven-failsafe is rejected on a specific technical ground**, not taste: the JaCoCo `check` execution declares no `<dataFile>`, so it reads `target/jacoco.exec`. Moving them writes coverage to `jacoco-it.exec` and the gate silently keeps measuring surefire only.
- Phase 1 is six hours and makes CI **faster** — `ci.yml` re-runs FindSecBugs that `mvn verify` already ran at the `verify` phase (`pom.xml:392`), worth −38s. Total ≈ 132 developer-hours across 8 phases; phases 1–4 close three defect categories and make every release verified and reversible.
- **Two authorisation divergences surfaced in passing and are left as decisions, not tests.** `SecurityConfig` gates `/api/**` at `.authenticated()` only, and neither `OutcomeController` nor `TideController` carries a method-level `@PreAuthorize` — so any authenticated user can `POST /api/outcome` (CLAUDE.md documents LITE_USER as read-only) and read every tide endpoint (documented under "Tides (ADMIN)"). The intent needs settling before any test locks in current behaviour.
- **§7 is candid about what the plan does not cover.** `actual_outcome` still holds zero rows, so forecast accuracy remains unvalidated and the calibration gate's `missedOpportunities`/`wastedTrips` counters stay inert; `PromptBuilder.java`, every threshold constant and every golden-file diff are named as the files no gate lets you stop reading.

## [v2.16.8] - 2026-07-26

### Changed — Production data moved off the macOS-shaped /Users path
- **`docker-compose.yml` bind-mounted the production database at `/Users/gregochr/goldenhour-data/postgres` on an Ubuntu host.** Host timestamps show the data actually started at `/home/gregochr/goldenhour-data` (28 Mar) and was moved to the macOS-shaped path on 12 Apr, leaving a stale 122 MB copy behind at the original location — so this is a move *back*, and the destination is not empty. Linux creates that path happily, so it has always worked — the cost is that the production database lives somewhere nobody would think to look. That is not a cosmetic complaint: the same stale macOS assumption is what let the nightly backup fail unnoticed for 132 nights, and a rollback touches this directory. Now `/home/gregochr/goldenhour-data/...`, along with the logs mount and `deploy.yml`'s `mkdir`.
- **`scripts/migrate-pgdata-path.sh` performs the move, and is built around the one way this goes badly.** If a compose file points at a path with no database, Postgres does not fail — it runs `initdb` and starts a **brand new empty one**, the app comes up healthy against it and begins writing. Any window where the compose file and the data disagree therefore looks exactly like total data loss, and the deploy now materialises `docker-compose.yml` from the released tag, which widens that window to "any release".
- **So the script removes the window rather than sequencing around it**: after moving, it leaves a **symlink** at the old path, so old and new compose files resolve to the same directory and the repo change can merge and deploy in any order. Gates before it touches anything: source must contain `PG_VERSION` (a real PGDATA), destination must be absent or empty, and a gzip-valid backup under 24h old must exist. It records row counts before and after and fails loudly if they differ — the signature of Postgres having initialised an empty database. Defaults to a dry run; `--apply` is explicit.
- **`deploy.yml` now refuses to start Postgres against a directory holding no database.** This is the most destructive failure available on that path and it is silent: pointed at an empty or missing directory, Postgres does not error — it runs `initdb`, starts a brand new empty database, and the backend comes up *healthy* against it and begins writing, while the real data sits untouched elsewhere. The guard reads the PGDATA bind source out of the compose file just materialised from the tag and verifies `PG_VERSION` before anything starts, so the migration and the repo change are now genuinely safe in **either** order rather than only one. (An earlier claim that the symlink alone made the ordering safe was wrong: it protects migrate-then-merge, not merge-then-migrate.) Applies only to bind mounts; a named volume cannot fail this way and is skipped.
- **The migration script must run as root and now says so.** A Postgres data directory is mode 700 owned by uid 70, so an unprivileged user cannot stat `PG_VERSION` inside it — the first version reported "does not look like a Postgres data directory" on a perfectly good one. It also resolves `COMPOSE_DIR`/`BACKUP_DIR` from `SUDO_USER` rather than `$HOME`, which under `sudo` is `/root`.
- Not changed: `backend/docker-compose.yml` still carries a `/Users` path, but it is dead H2-era config (`jdbc:h2:file:/data/goldenhour`) referenced by no workflow or script. Worth deleting separately — it declares `container_name: goldenhour-backend`, so running it would collide with the production container.

### Changed — CodeQL quality backlog: 48 alerts fixed, 18 deliberately left
- **Jackson 3 migration debt (16 alerts).** `JsonNode.asText()` was deprecated in Jackson 3 in favour of `asString()`. Safe to rename because it is a compiler-level alias, verified rather than assumed: `asText()` is `final` and delegates straight to `asString()`, so null/missing/non-textual semantics cannot change. Note `ClaudeAuroraInterpreter:198` has an `asText` that is **not** `JsonNode` — it is the Anthropic SDK's `ContentBlock` — so a file-wide find/replace would have broken the aurora path at compile time. 17 further sites remain in five files outside this pass.
- **Spring 7 deprecation (4 alerts).** `ResponseEntity.unprocessableEntity()` → `unprocessableContent()`; RFC 9110 renamed the 422 reason phrase. Both constants still resolve to 422.
- **Orphaned Javadoc (13 alerts) — one defect repeated three times, not thirteen drifts.** In each file a method had been inserted *between* a Javadoc block and the method it documented, so the block silently re-bound to the wrong signature. All 11 `ForecastService` tags were accurate descriptions of `buildEntity`, merely attached to `publishEvent` — deleting them, the obvious fix, would have destroyed correct documentation and left `buildEntity` undocumented. Each block was reunited with its method and the interposed methods documented, so coverage went up; `OutcomeService.record` is public and had been left with none at all.
- **Scraper parsing (8 alerts).** `NlcSightingClient` now routes every `Integer.parseInt` through the `parseIntOrNull` helper that already existed beside it. To be clear, the alerts were **false positives** — every capture group is bounded (`\d{1,2}`, `\d{4}`), so overflow is unreachable today. The change was made for a better reason than the alert gave: that safety rested on an invisible coupling between a regex quantifier at the top of the file and a parse call 170 lines below, so relaxing `FIRST_DAY` to `(\d+)` for a changed upstream layout — a plausible edit on a scraper — would have silently armed a mid-scrape crash.
- **Entity `toString` (4 alerts).** Not `@Data`/`@ToString` but Lombok's `@Builder` toString, which ignores `@ToString.Exclude` — so the only fix that works is giving the offending field type a real `toString`. One 1-line `toString` on `LocationEntity` cleared three alerts and is independently useful.
- **The activation guard reads as a named question.** `activateUser`'s `!"".equals(user.getPassword())` became `isAwaitingActivation(user)`, which states the rule it enforces instead of leaving the reader to infer it from a yoda comparison. The null-safety the yoda form provided is kept explicitly (`password != null && password.isEmpty()`), and the last CodeQL alert on the security fix goes with it.
- **18 alerts deliberately left, with reasons recorded at each site.** Five `unused-parameter` alerts are structural (interface contracts — removing the `Visitor` parameters would break every implementor); two `local-variable-is-never-read` alerts are Java 21 sealed-switch pattern bindings, where the only way to silence them is `default ->`, surrendering the compiler's exhaustiveness check; `AppUserEntity`'s missing `@Override` sits on Lombok-generated getters implementing *abstract* `UserDetails` methods, where the annotation adds no checking. A note-severity alert is a suggestion, not an instruction, and driving the dashboard to zero by making the code worse is a net loss.

### Fixed — Anyone could take over the admin account with one unauthenticated request
- **`POST /api/auth/set-password` was anonymous, accepted a raw `userId` from the request body, and set that account's password.** `SecurityConfig` permits `/api/auth/**`, `RegistrationService.setPasswordAndActivate` was a bare pass-through, and `UserService.activateUser` did `findById(userId)` → overwrite password → `setEnabled(true)` → `setPasswordChangeRequired(false)` with **no check that the account was awaiting activation and no check of the emailed verification token**. The response then auto-issued real tokens for that user, so a single request returned a working session.
- **The target was not a guess.** `V10` creates `app_user` with `IDENTITY` and inserts ADMIN as the first row, so the admin is `id = 1` on every deployment; ids are sequential after that. `{"userId": 1, "password": "…"}` yielded an ADMIN access token plus a 30-day refresh token — which survives a subsequent password reset, since only `refresh`/`logout` revoke a token family.
- **Turnstile was the only gate, and it gates the wrong thing.** `TurnstileService.verify` returns `true` outright when the secret is unset, so every local and test deployment needed no token at all; in production the sitekey is public and the widget is served on the app's own `/register` page. A CAPTCHA proves *a human sent this*, never *this human owns that mailbox*.
- **The proof-of-ownership step existed and was simply skipped.** `verifyEmail` hashes the emailed token, rejects unknown/used/expired, and marks it verified — then returns a bare integer that the next call accepted verbatim. `EmailVerificationTokenEntity` was never read again. The handshake was sound; nothing enforced that you had completed it.
- **Fixed in two independent layers, either of which closes the takeover.** `activateUser` now refuses any account that is not a pending self-registration (the sole shape `createPendingUser` produces: `enabled=false`, empty password), so a guessed id cannot touch live credentials. And `set-password` now resolves the account *from the one-time verification token* rather than from a caller-supplied id, requires it to be already verified and unexpired, and **deletes it on use** so it cannot be replayed. `AuthControllerTest` pins that a hostile `userId` riding along in the body never reaches `findById`.
- The CodeQL alert (`java/user-controlled-bypass`, #145) pointed at the auto-login return statement. That was a shadow of the real defect: the endpoint was an unauthenticated password-set primitive keyed on a guessable integer.

### Fixed — Log forging, ReDoS and unchecked arithmetic on anonymous endpoints (CodeQL)
- **23 log-injection sites.** Usernames, emails and request URIs reached log statements straight from anonymous request bodies, so a caller could embed CRLF and forge entire log lines — worst precisely where it landed, in the authentication audit trail. New `LogSanitizer` flattens control characters and bounds length; applied across `AuthenticationService`, `RegistrationService`, `UserEmailService`, `DynamicSchedulerService`, `SecurityConfig`, `RequestLoggingInterceptor` and the auth/waitlist controllers. Values that are provably safe (enums, numbers, DB-validated fields) are deliberately left unwrapped so the logs keep their signal.
- **4 polynomial-ReDoS patterns.** Three controllers each declared their own `^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$`, which backtracks quadratically on a long crafted input — on endpoints an anonymous caller can hit. Replaced by a shared `EmailValidator` whose pattern is unambiguous (`@` excluded from the local part, the dot excluded from each label) and therefore linear, behind an RFC 5321 length cap that bounds the work regardless. A timing test pins the property so a future "simplification" back to an ambiguous pattern is caught. Note it is marginally stricter than its predecessor: `a@b..com` and a trailing `a@b.com.` are now rejected.
- **Unchecked arithmetic on user-supplied paging.** `JobMetricsController` computed `size * (page + 1)` directly from query parameters; `size=2000000000&page=2000000000` overflowed the int. Page and size are now validated and clamped before any arithmetic.
- **`osascript` resolved via `PATH`**, so an earlier `PATH` entry could substitute a binary. Pinned to `/usr/bin/osascript`, and the AppleScript interpolation — which escaped quotes but not backslashes — now escapes in the correct order.

### Changed — CodeQL runs on pull requests, not only once a week
- **The scanner was working; nothing was reading it.** `codeql.yml` was `schedule` + `workflow_dispatch` only, so alerts accrued unseen: 79 on the first scan (2026-04-15) and a steady trickle since, none ever actioned. The clearest case is 2026-07-06, when the NLC scraper merge added **21 alerts in one go** that nobody saw for up to seven days.
- Added `pull_request` and `push` triggers so a merge cannot silently add alerts. The PR check reports only what the PR introduces, so a pre-existing backlog never blocks an unrelated change. The weekly schedule is **kept**, because a diff scan never re-examines untouched code — that is what catches a new CodeQL query finding an issue in code that has not changed.
- Added `.github/codeql/codeql-config.yml` excluding `target/` build output, `node_modules`, `dist`, scraper fixtures under `backend/src/test/resources` and generated `docs/`. 14 of the 118 open alerts were duplicates or fixtures; gating on a scanner that reports a defect in a captured third-party HTML fixture teaches people to click through the check. Test **code** is deliberately still scanned — only fixture data and generated output are dropped.

### Fixed — Adversarial review of the same day's estate work found five real defects
- **The release path was broken by the commit meant to make releases safer.** `pre-release-backup.sh` read `.RepoDigests` off a *container*; that field exists only on images, so the Go template errored, `2>/dev/null || true` swallowed both the error and the output, and the guard aborted the deploy **before** `docker compose pull`. Every subsequent release would have pushed a tag, built and published images, updated the CHANGELOG — and left production serving the old build. `deploy.yml` was also the only workflow in the repo with no failure notifier, so nothing would have said so. Fixed by hopping container → `.Image` → `RepoDigests`, and `deploy.yml` now has a `notify-failure` job like every other workflow.
- **The day's CSP and security headers would never have reached production.** `nginx.conf` is bind-mounted from the host's working copy (`frontend/Dockerfile` copies only `/app/dist`), and the deploy never updates that copy — so a release ships new images against whatever config is on disk. Worse, the ZAP scan mounts `nginx.conf` from the runner's *fresh* checkout, so it would have reported the headers fixed against a config production was not running: a false green of exactly the kind this work exists to remove. `deploy.yml` now materialises `nginx.conf` and `docker-compose.yml` from the released tag, using the idiom already justified three lines above it for the backup script.
- **The rollback "pin" was inert.** `rollback.sh` wrote `docker-compose.rollback.yml`, a name Compose never auto-loads — so the pin applied only to the single command that named it with `-f`, and the next bare `docker compose up -d` would quietly resolve `:latest` back to the bad release while the banner and the file on disk both still claimed production was pinned. Renamed to `docker-compose.override.yml`, and `deploy.yml` now refuses to deploy while a pin exists rather than silently re-applying it.
- **A partial restore reported success.** `rollback.sh` piped the dump into `psql` with neither `ON_ERROR_STOP` nor `--single-transaction`, so psql skipped what it could not apply, exited 0, and the script printed "ROLLBACK COMPLETE" over a database that was neither the old schema nor the new one. Both flags added: a failed restore now leaves the database exactly as it was and says so.
- **The pen-test stack ran production's scheduler with live API keys.** The restored copy carries production's `scheduler_job_config` rows, all ACTIVE, and the backend runs `SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod` against the production `.env` — so a forecast or tide job firing inside the scan window spent real money and made real outbound calls, none of it ZAP traffic. `zap.yml` now pauses every scheduled job in the copy before the backend starts.
- Review method: six reviewers over the changed surfaces, then one skeptic per finding instructed to **refute by default** — 24 raw findings, 12 verified, 5 survived. The refuted majority included several plausible-sounding claims about the backup script and CSP that did not withstand reading the code.

### Fixed — Security headers on the frontend (the first findings ZAP has ever surfaced)
- **Six of the eight ZAP frontend warnings are now fixed**, and they only became visible at all because the frontend scan had never once connected before today — it had reported "Connection refused" every run. Added on the two locations that serve documents and assets: `Content-Security-Policy`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `X-Frame-Options: DENY`, `Referrer-Policy`, `Permissions-Policy` (geolocation/camera/microphone/payment/usb all denied — none are used), `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy`, plus `server_tokens off` so nginx stops advertising its version.
- **The headers are repeated per-location on purpose.** nginx's `add_header` inheritance is a trap: a location defining *any* `add_header` of its own inherits *none* from the level above. Both `/assets/` and `/` set `Cache-Control`, so a tidy server-level block would have been present in the config and absent on the wire — the exact shape of "looks done, does nothing" this project has spent the day removing. Not applied to `/api/` or `/actuator/`, which proxy to Spring Security and already set their own.
- **The CSP was derived from a real production build, not guessed.** `script-src` needs no `'unsafe-inline'`: the build emits exactly one inline script — the `<script nomodule>` legacy-browser notice — which is allow-listed by SHA-256 hash, and the PWA registration turned out to be external (`/registerSW.js`). Sources were verified by extracting every external host from `frontend/dist` and checking each: `challenges.cloudflare.com` (Turnstile script + iframe), `*.basemaps.cartocdn.com` (Leaflet tiles), `nominatim.openstreetmap.org` (browser-side geocoding). Hosts appearing only inside minified error strings (Immer, React, Redux) and attribution `href`s (carto.com, leafletjs.com) were confirmed inert and deliberately not allow-listed.
- **Two findings are deliberately left standing.** `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy` (90004) — `require-corp` blocks any cross-origin subresource lacking CORP, which is both the Turnstile iframe and every basemap tile; breaking the map to satisfy a scanner is the wrong trade. And "Storable but Non-Cacheable Content" (10049), which describes the deliberate `Cache-Control: no-cache` revalidation strategy the ETag work depends on. "Modern Web Application" (10109) is informational, not a defect.

### Added — Pre-release backup and a rollback that knows what it is rolling back to
- **A release now dumps the database before it deploys**, from a new step in `deploy.yml` — *not* from `release.sh`. `release.sh` runs on a laptop and only pushes a tag; it cannot reach `goldenhour-db`, and a dump taken at tag time would predate the deploy by the ten minutes CI and the image build take. The backup belongs where the deploy actually happens.
- **The rollback problem was `:latest`.** `docker compose pull` overwrites it, so after a bad release `:latest` *is* the bad release and there is nothing left to roll back to. `pre-release-backup.sh` therefore records the running **image digests** — immutable, and readable straight off the containers — alongside the dump and the Flyway schema version, in a per-release manifest under `pre-release/`. Not `daily/`, whose 7-day rotation would delete the dump you want after a regression that took a fortnight to surface.
- **The dump and the digests are only meaningful as a pair.** Flyway is forward-only, so running the previous image against a migrated schema is not a rollback — it is a combination that has never been tested. `rollback.sh` restores both together, or refuses.
- **The pre-release backup aborts the release if it fails**, deliberately unlike the nightly off-box copy: this is the one moment a backup is load-bearing. It also refuses to write a manifest when an image has no digest (a locally-built image), rather than leaving something that looks complete and cannot be rolled back to.
- **`rollback.sh` is built for the once-in-two-years case.** Run with no arguments it lists the available rollback points; given a version it prints the full plan — digests, schema before and after, and an explicit data-loss warning — and requires the version typed back before touching anything. It dumps the current state first so the rollback is itself reversible, stops the app but leaves the database up (`--clean` DROPs fail against live connections), restores, then pins the old digests through a compose **override** that stays on disk so a stray `docker compose up` cannot silently restore the broken build.
- The deploy fetches the backup script from the released tag rather than the host's working copy, because nothing in the deploy ever runs `git pull` — the checkout on that host can be arbitrarily old, and a stale backup script is precisely the failure this exists to prevent.

### Added — CI failure notification, and a ZAP scan that actually authenticates
- **Nothing told anyone the gauntlet was down.** PIT and ZAP were red every Monday for four weeks in silence; the workflows are scheduled, so no one is watching a run list when they fail. New reusable `ci-failure-issue.yml` (`workflow_call`) opens a GitHub issue on failure and is wired into ZAP, PIT, CodeQL and the OWASP scan. It **runs on `ubuntu-latest`, not the self-hosted runner** — the likeliest cause of a self-hosted job failing is the self-hosted runner being unwell, and a notifier sharing that fate reports nothing. One open issue per workflow, with repeat failures appended as comments, so four red Mondays produce one issue with four comments rather than four issues nobody triages.
- **ZAP now scans the authenticated API surface.** It was running `zap-full-scan.py` against the root URL of a JSON API — which has no links to follow — so it spidered **6 URLs**, collected 500s from `/`, `/robots.txt` and `/sitemap.xml`, and never touched `/api/**` (correctly 401 behind `requestMatchers("/api/**").authenticated()`). Replaced with `zap-api-scan.py -f openapi` against `/v3/api-docs`, carrying a bearer token: the spec supplies the real endpoint list instead of relying on discovery.
- **The scan user is seeded per run, so no credential is stored anywhere.** A random password is generated each run, hashed with BCrypt via `htpasswd -B` in a throwaway container, and inserted straight into the ephemeral pen-test database — no new GitHub secret, nothing to rotate, and it dies with the stack. The hash reaches psql as a bound variable rather than string-interpolated SQL, because BCrypt hashes are full of `$` that an unquoted heredoc would expand as positional parameters and silently corrupt. The bearer token is passed to ZAP through a config **file** on a mount separate from the reports directory: the packaged scans split `-z` on whitespace so `Bearer <token>` cannot survive as one argument, and this also keeps the token out of the uploaded artifact.
- **The scan user is `PRO_USER`, deliberately not `ADMIN`.** An active scan carrying an admin token would POST `/api/forecast/run` and the other admin triggers on every run — real Claude spend and real outbound calls. `PRO_USER` covers the whole authenticated read surface, and scanning it also exercises the thing most worth proving: that ADMIN endpoints reject a non-admin token with 403 rather than leaking.
- **The pen-test stack no longer publishes any host ports.** The dispatched run died on `Bind for 0.0.0.0:8086 failed: port is already allocated` — `vbgone-frontend-1`, an unrelated project on the same host, has held 8086 since before this scan last worked. Moving to another free port would only defer the next collision on a shared box, so ZAP now joins `pentest_default` and addresses containers by service name (`pentest-backend:8082`, `pentest-frontend:80`) instead of `--network host` plus `localhost`. Publishing nothing is also the safer posture: the stack holds a restored copy of the production database and is about to be actively attacked, so it has no business being reachable from the host. Readiness probes use a throwaway `curlimages/curl` container on the same network, assuming no tooling inside the application images.
- **Three pen-test-only environment overrides**, because `docker-compose.pentest.yml` loads the **production** `.env`. `SPRINGDOC_API_DOCS_ENABLED=true` (the prod profile disables the OpenAPI spec, and without it there is nothing for ZAP to import); `TURNSTILE_SECRET_KEY=""` (blank makes `TurnstileService.verify()` return true, so login needs no CAPTCHA); and `SPRING_MAIL_HOST=localhost` on a dead port — `/api/auth/register` and `/api/auth/resend-verification` are `permitAll`, so an active scan **will** post to them, and against the real credentials in `.env` that would mean live mail from `noreply@photocast.online` to fuzzed addresses.

### Fixed — The weekly security gauntlet: two red workflows, and one that was green without checking anything
- **PIT mutation testing, red every Monday since 2026-06-29.** `SkyRatingEvalTest` was added on 2026-06-27 and PIT broke on the very next scheduled run. The test is `@Tag("prompt-regression")` and surefire excludes that tag, but PIT does its own test discovery and — with `parseSurefireConfig=false` — cannot see surefire's `excludedGroups`. The exclusion was therefore duplicated by hand in the pitest plugin's `excludedTestClasses`, which listed the *other* two prompt-regression tests and drifted the moment a third arrived. PIT picked it up, its `@BeforeAll` threw (`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY must be set` — the runner has no key), and PIT aborted the entire run with "Mutation testing requires a green suite". **No mutation score has been produced for four weeks.** Now excluded by JUnit **tag** via pitest's own `excludedGroups`, so a newly added tagged test is covered automatically, with the explicit class list retained as a fallback.
- **ZAP, red every Monday since 2026-06-29 — from a latent race, not a code change** (neither `zap.yml` nor `docker-compose.pentest.yml` had been touched since April). The readiness loop polled `pg_isready`, but while `initdb` runs the Postgres entrypoint serves a temporary server on the unix socket, so `docker exec … pg_isready` answers "accepting connections" **before `POSTGRES_DB` has been created**. The run log shows the restore failing 80 ms after the ready signal, on a freshly created volume: `FATAL: database "goldenhour" does not exist`. Both the workflow loop and the compose healthcheck now query the target database itself, which is only answerable once initdb has finished.
- **And ZAP was never meaningfully green even when it passed.** In the last "successful" run (2026-06-22): the **frontend scan reached nothing** — `Job spider failed to access URL http://localhost:8181 : Connection refused` — because the shared repo-root `nginx.conf` proxies to `goldenhour-backend`, a name that does not resolve in the pentest project, so nginx exits at startup with "host not found in upstream". The **backend scan crawled 6 URLs** and got HTTP 500 on `/`, `/robots.txt` and `/sitemap.xml`, i.e. it never got past the front door of a JWT-protected API. Both scans end in `|| true`, so findings can never fail the job — leaving *infrastructure* as its only real failure mode, and the readiness loops fell through silently when a target never came up. The loops now fail loudly and dump container logs, and `pentest-backend` carries a `goldenhour-backend` network alias so the shared nginx config resolves unmodified.
- **The OWASP dependency scan could not fail.** `backend/pom.xml` deliberately arms a gate — `failBuildOnCVSS=7` — and `continue-on-error: true` in the workflow discarded it, so the job reported success whatever the scan found. Removed; the report upload is already `if: always()`, so a failing scan still publishes its report. The command also pinned `dependency-check-maven:12.2.1` in a shell string while the pom declared `12.2.2` — Dependabot bumps the pom but cannot see a version embedded in a `run:` block — so the scan ran an older plugin than configured. Now invoked by plugin prefix, resolving the pom's version, suppression file and threshold.
- **Not a fault: Dependabot.** 144 PRs, one batch every Monday without a gap (including 6 on 2026-07-20), zero open, all auto-merged. The absence of visible PRs is `dependabot-auto-merge.yml` working — minor and patch bumps enable auto-merge and land without ever resting in the review queue. Worth knowing that those merges gate on the CI workflow only: CodeQL, ZAP, PIT and the OWASP scan are schedule-only and are not required checks, so no dependency bump has ever been gated on them.

### Changed — Cloud verification backfill now batches archive requests (~20x fewer calls)
- **The first backfill run measured the problem: 24,638 evaluations left, at 2 archive calls each = ~49,000 requests.** At Open-Meteo's daily ceiling that is roughly a week of trickling, during which the capped-vs-uncapped split that answers D7 runs on a fraction of the data.
- **The archive accepts comma-separated coordinates and returns a JSON array**, exactly like the forecast batch endpoint (verified against the live API before building). New `OpenMeteoArchiveClient` batches `BATCH_COORD_LIMIT` (20) points per request, reusing `OpenMeteoClient`'s constant and request factory so chunk size and timeouts cannot drift between the two. `backfill` now groups candidates by date and lays points out two per candidate — horizon at `2i`, observer at `2i+1` — so one request covers both claims for a whole date.
- **~49,000 requests becomes ~2,470**, turning a multi-day trickle into a single session.
- Failure handling preserves **positional alignment**, which is the safety-critical property: a failed chunk nulls only its own positions and the returned list always matches the requested length. A short list would silently shift every subsequent evaluation's observations onto the wrong row — swapping a gap reading for a canvas one rather than failing loudly. Tests pin length and order across chunk boundaries, partial failures, unparseable bodies, and the single-object response Open-Meteo returns for one coordinate.

### Fixed — Backup verification condemned a good backup on its first run
- **The row-count check used `location`; the table is `locations`.** `psql` errored with "relation does not exist", `|| echo 0` swallowed it, and the result surfaced as "Restored backup has no rows in location — the dump is not usable". The restore had in fact worked perfectly: 31,072 rows in `forecast_evaluation` and 4 in `app_user` from the same dump.
- **The real defect was the masking, not the typo.** `|| echo 0` collapsed *query failed* and *table is empty* into one indistinguishable outcome, so a mistake in the check was indistinguishable from a corrupt backup — the failure mode most likely to make you stop believing the alarm. The two are now reported separately: a non-zero `psql` exit is "could not query … the restore is incomplete or the table is missing" with the actual error text, and only a genuine zero count reports an unusable dump.
- The notifier behaved correctly throughout, opening a `CI: Backup Verification is failing` issue from `ubuntu-latest`. The alarm worked; the check was wrong.

### Changed — Off-box backup copy: redundancy failure is now its own signal
- **The off-box copy no longer fails the whole backup job.** It was fatal, which sounds strict but is the wrong severity: by that point the local dump is written and integrity-checked, so a failed copy means *no redundancy*, not *no backup*. The off-box target is a Mac, which sleeps — so as written it would have gone red most nights, and a backup job that is habitually red is one you stop reading. That is precisely how the previous 132-night gap survived.
- **Not silent instead of not fatal.** The script writes `offbox-status` (`ok`/`failed` + timestamp) and `backup-verify.yml` reports on it as a **separate check** with its own message, distinguishing "the backup exists only on the Docker host" from "there is no backup". An absent status file means off-box copying is not configured, which is a warning rather than a failure. The check runs *after* the restore test so broken replication can never stop us proving the backup restores.
- rsync now runs with `ssh -o BatchMode=yes -o ConnectTimeout=15`, so an unattended run fails fast instead of blocking forever on a password or host-key prompt nobody is there to answer.
- Corrected a misstatement in the previous entry: `backup-verify.yml` does **not** key off `last-success`. It checks the age of the newest dump itself — verifying the artefact is stronger than trusting the job's own claim to have produced one. `last-success` remains as a human-readable "when did this last work end to end".

### Fixed — The database had not been backed up for 132 days
- **Backups silently stopped on 2026-03-16.** `launchctl list` showed `com.goldenhour.backup` loaded with last exit status **1**, and `/Users/gregochr/goldenhour-backups/daily/` held exactly one file: `goldenhour_2026-03-16.sql.gz`. The job was firing every night on the **Mac**, but Docker had moved to the Ubuntu host (`dockermacmini`, `docker context ls` → `unix:///var/run/docker.sock`), so `docker inspect goldenhour-db` failed, the script hit its own guard, exited 1, and wrote "Container is not running" to a log nobody read — 132 nights running. Not a crash, not an outage: a safety mechanism that had been reporting its own failure to an empty room. The same disease as the CI gauntlet, five times longer.
- **`scripts/backup-postgres.sh` rewritten** to stop hardcoding a host. Paths now default to `${HOME}` and are overridable; it refuses to run where `docker`/`goldenhour-db` are absent with an error naming this exact failure. Dumps are written to `.partial` and `gzip -t`-verified before being renamed, so an interrupted or truncated run can never leave something that looks like a backup. New `OFFBOX_DEST` copies each dump to separate hardware — backups sharing a disk with the database are one failure from useless. Fixed along the way: `log()` wrote to stdout, so `DAILY_COUNT=$(rotate …)` captured log lines instead of a number; it now logs to stderr, and the summary recounts after rotating rather than reporting the pre-rotation figure.
- **`goldenhour-backup.service` + `.timer` replace the launchd plist**, which has been deleted — it encoded a topology that no longer exists and is precisely what failed. `Persistent=true` so a window missed while the host is down is made up rather than skipped, and `TimeoutStartSec=30min` so a hung dump fails instead of looking like success forever.
- **`backup-verify.yml` checks the backup from the outside, every morning.** It asserts a dump exists and is under 48h old, then **restores the newest one into a scratch Postgres container and counts rows** in `forecast_evaluation`, `location` and `app_user`, failing if any is empty. A file existing proves nothing — a truncated dump, a dump of an empty database and a good backup are all plausibly-sized `.sql.gz`. Deliberately independent of the backup job, because the last 132 days are the argument against trusting a component to report its own failure; failure opens a GitHub issue via a new reusable `ci-failure-issue.yml`, which the scheduled security scans will share once that work lands. Its readiness loop queries the target database rather than `pg_isready`, because during initdb Postgres answers `pg_isready` on the unix socket before `POSTGRES_DB` exists.
- **README corrected.** It described the production database as H2, volume-mounted and "covered by Time Machine" — untrue since the Postgres migration, and believing it is part of how the gap went unnoticed.

## [v2.16.7] - 2026-07-25

### Added — Cloud verification: validate forecasts against reanalysis, with no human needed (V129)
- **`actual_outcome` is empty — zero rows, ever.** Nobody has recorded what a sky did, so every scoring rule is unvalidated, including the cloud-approach veto that forces rating 1–2 on a large share of evaluations. The calibration gate can't help while there is nothing to calibrate against, and the barrier is real rather than a UI problem: a summer sunrise at 04:30 is not a thing anyone does routinely.
- **But the rules are cloud claims, and cloud is machine-checkable.** Every threshold they turn on — solar-horizon low cloud >60% blocks light, a 20 pp build means the event-time snapshot understates cloud, a 30 pp drop at 226 km means a thin strip — is a statement about cloud, not about beauty. New `OpenMeteoArchiveApi` (Open-Meteo historical/reanalysis) plus `CloudVerificationService` fetch the cloud that *actually* occurred and score the forecast against it, **retroactively over evaluations already in the database**.
- **Two points per evaluation, matching the two claims a forecast makes** — the *gap* (low cloud at the 113 km solar horizon: did the low sun get through?) and the *canvas* (mid/high cloud overhead at the observer: was there a screen to light up?). A forecast can be wrong about either independently, and they fail differently, so a single blended error would hide both.
- **`GET /api/admin/cloud-verification` (ADMIN)** reports gap and canvas error split three ways, each chosen to answer an open question: `vetoFired` vs `vetoNotFired` (when the veto forced 1–2★, was the horizon really blocked? `gapActuallyOpen` counts the clear skies it suppressed); **`vetoUncapped` vs `vetoCapped`** (below the 200 km cap the upwind sample is a genuine advection nowcast, at the cap it is not — if the veto only tracks reality when uncapped, the cap is the dominant defect, D7); and `byWindSunAngle` (whether observer-anchored upwind sampling misfires when wind and sun align).
- **`POST /api/admin/cloud-verification/backfill?limit=` (ADMIN)** works the backlog in bounded, resumable passes. Candidates are selected by anti-join and *every* attempt writes a row — including one that found no archive data — so nothing is fetched twice. Only target dates older than the archive's ~6-day lag are eligible. Observations are read at the same `findBestIndex` slot the forecast scored, so like is compared with like.

### Fixed — The cloud-approach veto could silently lose one of its two triggers
- **The upwind sample was placed with one wind reading and looked up with another.** The batch and sync prefetchers chose the steering wind at `TimeSlotUtils.findNearestIndex`, while `CloudPointCacheReader` re-derives the same point from the wind `extractAtmosphericData` selected at `findBestIndex`. Those rules disagree for roughly **half of all events** — `findBestIndex` is target-type aware and takes the latest slot at or before a sunset (the post-sunset slot has no radiation), so a 21:37 sunset resolves to 21:00 while `findNearestIndex` picks 22:00. A different hour means a different bearing, a different coordinate, and — because the cache is a grid-snapped exact-match lookup — a **miss**: `upwindSample` came back null and the cloud-approach veto lost its `upwind current ≥60%` trigger. No exception, no log line. At the 200 km cap a bearing difference of only ~3° is enough to cross a 0.1° cache cell.
- **`OpenMeteoResponseParser.resolveEventWind` is now the single decider** of that wind, and both prefetchers call it instead of carrying near-identical inline copies. Its Javadoc states the invariant and why it must not drift, since the failure mode is silent and the two computations sit in different packages.
- Consequence worth noting: the veto's observed firing rate (~15% of evaluations) was measured under this defect, so it reflects when the two slot rules happened to agree rather than when the meteorological conditions were met.

### Changed — Plan tab: two N+1 query loops collapsed onto the existing indexed bulk query
- **`GET /api/briefing` and `GET /api/briefing/evaluate/scores` each issued roughly one database round trip per enabled location** — ~50 today — and every one of those queries returned *all* historical runs over the 15-day window from an insert-only table, materialising the full 36-column entity (two of them TEXT) just to keep the newest row per slot and discard the rest. Both now use the single dedup-at-source query that `GET /api/forecast` already uses, backed by the `idx_forecast_eval_latest_run` composite index (V128): one indexed statement instead of N range scans, with the winnowing done in SQL rather than by pulling the whole history into memory
- Three behaviours are deliberately **retained**, because the bulk query is not a drop-in on its own: the `types` filter (the query does not filter target type, so `HOURLY` rows still have to be dropped by the caller), the strict-`isAfter` in-memory reduction (the `forecastRunAt = (SELECT MAX(...))` predicate returns *both* rows on an exact timestamp tie, so the reduction keeps the first row seen exactly as before — a `Collectors.toMap` would throw on the duplicate key), and iteration over the location list rather than the query result (so the cached-score-wins precedence resolves in the same stable order). An explicit empty-list guard mirrors the one in `ForecastController`, since an empty `IN` list is invalid on some dialects
- Test coverage was migrated rather than relaxed: the existing latest-run-wins, HOURLY-filtering, cached-wins, triage-fallback and mixed-source assertions all still hold (the latest-run case now exercises the retained in-memory reduction directly), the query-shape test now asserts **exactly one** call carrying every location id — and that neither superseded query shape is used — and a new case covers the empty-list guard

### Added — Forecast calibration gate: accuracy measured against recorded outcomes
- **The project had no way to tell whether a scoring change made forecasts more or less accurate.** The prompt-regression tests compare Claude to hand-written expectations, the sky-rating eval harness compares band assignments to fixtures, and the model-comparison harness compares models to each other — all three are self-referential and can stay green while the forecast drifts away from reality. `actual_outcome` held the recorded observations, but nothing scored forecasts against them ("prediction accuracy feedback" was still a Planned item).
- **New `GET /api/admin/calibration?from=&to=` (ADMIN)** joins `forecast_evaluation` to `actual_outcome` on the natural key both share — location, date, target type — and reports accuracy **overall, per forecast horizon, and per model**. Defaults to a trailing 90-day window. `ForecastCalibrationService` keeps only the newest run within each (slot, horizon) group, so a slot re-evaluated by both the nightly batch and the intraday refresh is not weighted twice.
- **Reports decision errors, not just distance from truth.** Alongside signed mean error (which separates optimism from pessimism — mean *absolute* error cannot), exact-match and within-one rates, each bucket counts **missed opportunities** (predicted ≤ 2, actual ≥ 4 — told to stay home on a good sky) and **wasted trips** (predicted ≥ 4, actual ≤ 2). An absolute rating ceiling in the prompt can only ever create missed opportunities, which makes that count the direct before/after gate on relaxing the cloud-approach veto (F2).
- Outcomes recorded with `wentOut = false` still count — a photographer can rate a sky they watched from a window — so the flag is projected rather than filtered on; the only admission requirement is that both ratings are present. Aggregation is deterministic for a given window, so two runs over the same window diff directly.

### Fixed — Cloud-approach trend now reads the whole solar cone, not one grid cell (F1)
- **Two locations 10 km apart could differ by three stars for the same sunset.** A production case (Angel of the North 1★/fiery 15 vs Copt Hill 4★/fiery 72, same region, same batch, same `evaluated_at`) traced to the combined-signal rule in `PromptBuilder` — the only rule that forces a rating **regardless of all other evidence** ("output rating 1 or 2, fiery_sky ≤20… even if the solar horizon appears clear, canvas is present, or aerosol is favourable"). Its `[BUILDING]` trigger was read from a **single un-coned point** at 113 km, while the solar-horizon cloud reading at the *same bearing and distance* is averaged over a 3-point cone precisely to "smooth out Open-Meteo grid-cell boundary effects" (`DirectionalSamplingGeometry`). The most absolute rule in the system was running on the least smoothed input.
- **The trend is now averaged across the same three cone bearings.** New `SolarCloudTrend.average(...)` combines the per-bearing trajectories slot-by-slot; `isBuilding()`/`isClearing()` then evaluate the smoothed trajectory. Mid/high average only when every contributing slot captured them, preserving the "canvas not captured" semantics `isClearing()` relies on.
- **Free on the batch path.** `computeSolarHorizonPoint` returns exactly the centre of the cone that `computeDirectionalCloudPoints` already fetched, so the two flanking samples were sitting unused in `CloudPointCache` — the smoothing costs **no extra Open-Meteo request**. The live-fetch twin (`OpenMeteoService.fetchCloudApproachData`) now cones too, so the batch and synchronous engines cannot drift apart; it adds 2 coords to an already-batched request.
- Cone bearings are now single-homed in `DirectionalSamplingGeometry.solarConeBearings()`/`computeSolarConePoints()` (they were duplicated in `CloudPointCacheReader`). Regression test: a centre-bearing spike that trips `isBuilding()` on its own is damped below the 20 pp threshold by the cone, while a build all three bearings agree on still fires.

### Fixed — Batch evaluations left no record of what Claude returned (F4)
- **A batch-scored slot was undiagnosable after the fact.** `api_call_log.request_body` and `response_body` were both null for every batch row — the raw response was persisted only when the regex fallback path fired — so a suspect score could not be audited at all. `persistBatchLog` now stores the raw response on the clean-success path too, for both the forecast and bluebell batch parsers. `response_body` is already `TEXT` and the Job Run screen already surfaces it. (The *prompt* side remains unstored — `logBatchResult` has no request-body parameter; that is a separate change.)

### Added — ETag coverage for the Plan tab's own endpoints (the actual hot path)
- **The Plan tab's endpoints now revalidate as 304s.** The earlier ETag work was scoped around the map's endpoints, so the screen that actually loads on every visit — Best Bet, Hot Topics, region rows, the astro grid — was still re-downloading its full payload on every background revalidation. Added: `/api/briefing` (mount + 10-minute poll + **every window focus**), `/api/briefing/evaluate/scores` (the largest payload — all locations × 15 days × 2 events, each with a Claude summary), `/api/astro/conditions` (+ `/available-dates`), `/api/aurora/status` (5-minute poll + focus) and `/api/nlc/sighting` (10-minute poll + focus)
- **Removed two unreachable entries** (`/api/forecast/history`, `/api/forecast/compare`) — no frontend caller exists, and leaving them in made the list read as load-bearing when it wasn't
- **Deliberately still excluded, now documented in the config:** `/api/travel-days` (carries an optional free-text note plus absence dates) and `/api/user/settings[/drive-times]` (home postcode / lat-lon), alongside the existing outcome exclusion. Same reasoning throughout — an ETag needs `private, no-cache`, which lets the browser persist the body to an on-disk cache that JavaScript **cannot** evict on logout, so anything user-authored or home-derived stays on `no-store`. Admin/interaction-only reads are omitted as well: buffering cost, no repeat-fetch payoff
- **Verified the optimisation actually fires:** a test asserts two unchanged `GET /api/briefing` reads produce the **identical** ETag. The briefing is re-derived per request (hot topics, aurora overlay), so a single `now()`-derived field anywhere in it would mean a guaranteed 200 on every poll and no saving at all. Also covered: the cold-start `204` still returns 204, and a dedicated `HttpCachingConfigTest` pins the path predicate — **all three SSE streams and `/api/briefing/evaluate` stay excluded**, the whitelist is exact-match (never a prefix), and the `/api/forecast/\d+` regex is strict
- Fixed a stale API list in `CLAUDE.md` that documented a `GET /api/briefing/evaluate` SSE endpoint which no longer exists — an active hazard when scoping HTTP caching, since it sits directly beneath a path now being whitelisted

### Added — Load-program follow-ups: ETag on the popup-detail endpoint, installable PWA manifest
- **`GET /api/forecast/{id}` (the lazy popup detail) is now ETag-revalidated too**, so re-opening a location's popup in a later session returns a cheap `304` instead of re-sending the full evaluation. It's matched by a strict `\d+` numeric-id pattern rather than a `/api/forecast/*` wildcard — a wildcard would also swallow the SSE streams at `/api/forecast/run/notifications` and `/run/{runId}/progress`, which must never be buffered. The filter's scope is now a single explicit path predicate (mapped broadly, then narrowed) rather than a URL-pattern list that has to be kept in sync with a wildcard's blast radius, and a regression test asserts **both SSE paths still receive no ETag** (#23)
- **A web-app manifest makes the app installable / Add-to-Home-Screen-able**, with proper 192×192 and 512×512 icons generated from the logo, an `apple-touch-icon` (iOS ignores the manifest for Home Screen icons), and `theme_color`/`background_color` matching the dark UI so the splash and status bar don't flash white on launch. Icons are `purpose: "any"` only — a `maskable` variant needs safe-zone padding the current logo doesn't have, and a wrongly-cropped adaptive icon is worse than none. Verified in a production build: the manifest links and parses, both icons serve `200 image/png`, and the service worker's `/api` isolation is unchanged (zero `/api` cache entries)

### Added — PWA app shell: the UI frame paints from disk on refresh
- **A service worker (`vite-plugin-pwa`) now precaches the app shell**, so a refresh renders the UI frame from disk instead of waiting on the network. Verified in a production build: after the first visit, the navigation **and all 9 shell assets are served by the service worker with zero network transfer** (#25)
- **Deliberately scoped precache** — only the critical shell (`index.html`, entry JS/CSS, the React vendor chunk, the runtime, and the 11 self-hosted font files; 17 entries). The heavy lazy chunks (recharts ~360K, ManageView ~251K, leaflet ~196K) are **excluded**, since precaching them would pull ~800K on every deploy for a user who may never open the Map or Manage views; they're runtime-cached `CacheFirst` on first actual use instead (safe because the filenames are content-hashed)
- **`/api` is never cached.** The stale-while-revalidate cache and ETags already own that, and a cached API response would be a stale-data and cross-account hazard — so those requests (including the SSE streams, which must never be buffered) pass straight through to the network. Verified: zero `/api` cache entries, and an `/api` request reaches the network rather than being answered with the SPA shell, while client-side routes still get the shell via the navigation fallback (with `/api` + `/actuator` denylisted)
- `registerType: 'autoUpdate'` plus `cleanupOutdatedCaches` so a deploy can't strand a user on a stale shell; the `no-cache` shell policy from #24 already makes `sw.js` itself always revalidate. No web-app manifest is declared — the existing icons aren't the sizes an installable PWA needs and this change is about load speed, not installability

### Changed — Map render performance: O(1) briefing-score lookups, memoised filters and marker icons
- **The per-marker briefing-score lookup is no longer an O(n²) scan.** `getBriefingScore` linear-scanned the whole scores Map for *every* marker on *every* render (O(markers × scores)); the scores are now indexed once per map into a hash keyed `date|eventType|locationName`, making each lookup O(1). The index keys off the score key's **last three fields** — exactly what the old `endsWith` suffix matched — so it stays faithful even if a (user-created) region name contains the `|` delimiter, and it preserves first-inserted-wins on a duplicate tail. Locked in by an equivalence test that asserts identity parity against the original scan across a full location × date × event matrix plus the delimiter, duplicate, empty and nullish edge cases (#35)
- **The filter pipeline and marker icons are memoised.** The type → rating → drive-time → dark-sky → visible chain now runs in `useMemo`s (with complete dependency arrays) instead of recomputing on every render, and built `DivIcon`s are cached by the content that determines them (name + scores + wildlife/cluster/stand-down flags, a superset of every icon input), so a zoom, hover, or filter change no longer rebuilds every marker's SVG. Sharing a `DivIcon` is Leaflet-safe — `createIcon()` builds fresh DOM per marker — and the options that cluster averaging reads are part of the cache key (#35, #36)
- **Marker keys are stable** (`loc.name` instead of a name+drive-time composite), so markers no longer all remount when drive times finish loading (#34)
- Deliberately not done: `useDeferredValue` on the filters (#8) would defer marker updates a tick behind the control state — a perceptible change for no gain now that filtering is O(n) with cached icons; zoom isolation (#37) is effectively subsumed since the marker set no longer depends on zoom; and per-resource loading in `useForecasts` (#5) is moot now that the SWR cache paints instantly

### Changed — Self-hosted fonts: no render-blocking Google Fonts round-trip
- **The IBM Plex Sans / IBM Plex Mono and Newsreader web fonts are now self-hosted** (bundled via `@fontsource`, latin subset) instead of loaded from a render-blocking Google Fonts `<link>` in `index.html`. The load path no longer waits on a third-party stylesheet round-trip; the fonts ship same-origin, content-hashed and immutable-cached (per #24). The `@font-face` family names match the existing `--font-*` `@theme` tokens, so no CSS change was needed, and the weights mirror the previous Google Fonts request exactly. Verified in a production build: IBM Plex Sans renders from the self-hosted woff2 with **zero** requests to `fonts.googleapis`/`fonts.gstatic` (#10)

## [v2.16.6] - 2026-07-25

### Changed — Slim forecast list DTO + lazy popup detail: the big payload win
- **`GET /api/forecast` now returns a slim `ForecastListDto` (~20 fields) instead of the full ~66-field DTO.** The map's list endpoint ships hundreds of rows (every enabled location × the date window); it now carries only what the markers, cluster averages, azimuth lines, date grouping, and comfort rows read — the ~40 heavier popup-only fields (the large Claude `summary` text, atmospherics, directional cloud, tide/surge/inversion detail, bluebell, golden/blue-hour windows) are gone from the list. This dramatically shrinks the payload, its server serialization cost, and the frontend's localStorage SWR-cache footprint (so instant-paint actually fits at 200+ locations). **The Plan tab is unaffected** — it renders from `/api/briefing` + `getAllEvaluationScores`, never the forecast DTO (#30)
- **New `GET /api/forecast/{id}` returns the full evaluation on demand**, fetched lazily by the map popup when it opens. Role-gating is identical to the list (LITE basic vs PRO/ADMIN enhanced scores + summary), so the detail endpoint can't be used to bypass the freemium gating. Sparse cached-only rows (future dates not yet persisted, `id == null`) carry their `summary`/`triageMessage` inline in the slim list, so their popups still show the "why" text without a by-id fetch
